The **Vietnam War** (c. 1955[^cite_note-start_date-1] – 30 April 1975) was an armed conflict in [Vietnam](202354.md), [Laos](17752.md), and [Cambodia](334751.md) fought between [North Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Vietnam) (Democratic Republic of Vietnam) and [South Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Vietnam) (Republic of Vietnam) and their allies. North Vietnam was supported by the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) and [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_in_the_Vietnam_War), while South Vietnam was supported by the [United States](3434750.md) and other [anti-communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism) nations. The conflict was the second of the [Indochina wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indochina_wars) and a [proxy war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_war) of the [Cold War](325329.md). The Vietnam War was one of the postcolonial [wars of national liberation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wars_of_national_liberation), a theater in the Cold War, and a [civil war](7085.md), with civil warfare a defining feature from the outset.[^cite_note-Departure-47] Direct [US military involvement](8453309.md) escalated from 1965 until US forces were withdrawn in 1973. The fighting spilled into the [Laotian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laotian_Civil_War) and [Cambodian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_Civil_War) civil wars, which ended with all three countries becoming [communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_state) in 1975.

The civil and colonial conflicts in Vietnam became internationalized in 1950.[^cite_note-advisors-52] After the defeat of the [French Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Union) in the [First Indochina War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Indochina_War), Vietnam's independence was affirmed at the [1954 Geneva Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Geneva_Conference), but the country was divided at the [17th parallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_Demilitarized_Zone): the [Viet Minh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viet_Minh), led by [Ho Chi Minh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho_Chi_Minh), took control of North Vietnam, while [Ngo Dinh Diem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngo_Dinh_Diem) led South Vietnam, which the US assumed financial and military support for. The North Vietnamese supplied and eventually directed the [Viet Cong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viet_Cong) (VC), a [communist front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_front) in the south that intensified a guerrilla war from 1957. In 1958, North Vietnam [invaded Laos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Vietnamese_invasion_of_Laos), establishing the [Ho Chi Minh trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho_Chi_Minh_trail) to supply the VC insurgency. By 1961, North Vietnam was covertly sending soldiers of its [People's Army of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Army_of_Vietnam) (PAVN) to assist the southern insurgents. President [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy) increased US involvement in the early 1960s, including [military advisors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Assistance_Advisory_Group) and aid to the [Army of the Republic of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_of_the_Republic_of_Vietnam) (ARVN). In 1963, Diem was killed in [a US-backed ARVN military coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_South_Vietnamese_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), which added to South Vietnam's growing instability.

Following the [Gulf of Tonkin incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Tonkin_incident) in 1964, the US Congress passed [a resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Tonkin_Resolution) that gave President [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) authority to increase military presence without declaring war. Johnson launched [a bombing campaign of the north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Rolling_Thunder) and deployed combat troops, dramatically increasing deployment to 184,000 by 1966, and 536,000 by 1969. US forces relied on [air supremacy](537478.md) and overwhelming firepower to conduct [search and destroy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Search_and_destroy) operations in rural areas. Communist forces relied on [guerrilla tactics](12720.md), using the countryside and jungle as concealed base areas.

In 1968, the communists under [Lê Duẩn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%AA_Du%E1%BA%A9n) launched the [Tet Offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tet_Offensive), which was a tactical defeat but contributed to growing American opposition to the war. Johnson's successor, [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon), began "[Vietnamization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamization)" from 1969, which saw the conflict fought by an expanded ARVN while US forces withdrew. The [1970 Cambodian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_Cambodian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) resulted in a PAVN invasion and US–ARVN [counter-invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_campaign), escalating its civil war.

With its ranks degraded by widespread drug abuse and plummeting morale, US troops had mostly withdrawn from Vietnam by 1972. However, American forces provided crucial air support to ARVN against North Vietnam's massive [Easter Offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Offensive) with the [Linebacker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Linebacker) [Operations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Linebacker_II). Following the 1973 [Paris Peace Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Peace_Accords), the last American forces left. The accords were subsequently violated by North Vietnam, and bloody fighting continued until the [1975 Spring Offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_spring_offensive). Weakened by years of corruption and the economic troubles of South Vietnam's [Thiệu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nguy%E1%BB%85n_V%C4%83n_Thi%E1%BB%87u) regime, [Saigon fell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Saigon) to the PAVN, marking the war's end. North and South Vietnam were officially reunified in 1976.

The war exacted an [enormous cost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War_casualties): estimates of Vietnamese soldiers and civilians killed range from 970,000 to 3 million. Some 275,000–310,000 Cambodians, 20,000–62,000 Laotians, and 58,220 US service members died.[^cite_note-USd\&w-33] The war was also marked by brutal atrocities, including large-scale [massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre) (such as [Huế](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre_at_Hu%E1%BA%BF) and [Mỹ Lai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/My_Lai_massacre)), terrorism, indiscriminate bombings, [rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rape_during_the_Vietnam_War), torture, and [persecution of ethnic minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_the_Montagnard_in_Vietnam). 20% of South Vietnam's jungle was sprayed with [toxic herbicides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agent_Orange), which led to significant health problems.[^cite_note-Westing-1984-56]

Political repression and flawed economic policies following the war would precipitate the [Vietnamese boat people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_boat_people) and the larger [Indochina refugee crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indochina_refugee_crisis),[^cite_note-57] which saw millions leave Indochina, of which about 250,000 perished at sea.[^cite_note-Henningfeld-58] The [Khmer Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_Rouge) carried out the [Cambodian genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_genocide), and the [Cambodian–Vietnamese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian%E2%80%93Vietnamese_War) began in 1978. In response, China [invaded Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Vietnamese_War), with [border conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Vietnamese_conflicts_\(1979%E2%80%931991\)) lasting until 1991. Within the US, the war gave rise to [Vietnam syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_syndrome), an aversion to American overseas military involvement,[^cite_note-59] which, with the [Watergate scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watergate_scandal), contributed to the crisis of confidence that affected the United States throughout the 1970s.[^cite_note-60]

## Names

Various names have been applied, though *Vietnam War* is the most commonly used in English. It has also been called the **Second Indochina War**, as it spread to Laos and Cambodia,[^cite_note-61] the *Vietnam Conflict*,[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63] and colloquially *'Nam*. [South Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Vietnam) used terms such as *Kháng chiến chống Cộng sản* (lit. 'Resistance War against Communists')[^cite_note-64] and *Cuộc chiến bảo vệ tự do* (lit. 'Fight to Protect Freedom').[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsselin2024a17-65] [North Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Vietnam) at the time, and official histories produced by the Government of Vietnam today, refer to it as *Kháng chiến chống Mỹ, cứu nước* (lit. 'Resistance War against America to save the nation'), or simply the *Resistance War against America*.[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67] Vietnamese both within the country and overseas occasionally refer to it as *Chiến tranh Việt Nam*, the Vietnam War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsselin2024a17-65]

## Background

Main articles: [French Indochina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Indochina), [Vietnamese nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_nationalism), and [Communism in Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism_in_Vietnam)

Vietnam had been under French control as part of [French Indochina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Indochina) since the 1880s. Vietnamese independence movements, such as the [Vietnamese Nationalist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vi%E1%BB%87t_Nam_Qu%E1%BB%91c_D%C3%A2n_%C4%90%E1%BA%A3ng), faced suppression despite growing public support for diverse reformist and revolutionary [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism) causes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETran20225–19-68][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoscha2016135–136,_170–181,_206-69] [Nguyen Sinh Cung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nguyen_Sinh_Cung) established the [Indochinese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indochinese_Communist_Party) (ICP) in 1930; the [Marxist–Leninist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism%E2%80%93Leninism) party aimed to overthrow French rule and establish a communist state.[^cite_note-Umair_Mirza-2017-70]

Fractures between nationalists and communists emerged in the late 1920s,[^cite_note-Reilly_ori-71] as the two groups differed in their visions for postcolonial Vietnam: [republicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism) for the revolutionary nationalists,[^cite_note-Tran_&_Vu_2022-72] and [proletarian internationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proletarian_internationalism) for the communists.[^cite_note-Vu_2019-73] The communists' radical push for centralized control led to a prolonged civil conflict marked by the suppression of rival nationalists,[^cite_note-Asselin_2023-74] with the ICP largely responsible for initiating systemic Vietnamese-on-Vietnamese violence.[^cite_note-75]<sup>: 515 </sup>

### Japanese occupation of Indochina

Main articles: [French Indochina in World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Indochina_in_World_War_II) and [1940–1946 in French Indochina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1940%E2%80%931946_in_French_Indochina)

In September 1940, [Japan invaded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_invasion_of_French_Indochina) French Indochina, following France's [capitulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_France) to [Nazi Germany](21212.md). By 1941, Japan had gained full military access across Indochina and established a dual colonial rule that preserved [Vichy French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vichy_France) administration while facilitating Japanese military operations.[^cite_note-76] Cung, now known as [Ho Chi Minh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho_Chi_Minh), returned from exile to establish the anti-Japanese [Viet Minh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viet_Minh) movement.[^cite_note-Umair_Mirza-2017-70] In 1945, the US [Office of Strategic Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Strategic_Services) (O.S.S.) provided the Viet Minh with training and weapons to develop local intelligence networks against Japanese forces.[^cite_note-77][^cite_note-Kinzer-2013-78]

In March 1945, Japan, losing the war, [overthrew the French government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_in_French_Indochina) in Indochina, established the [Empire of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Vietnam), and maintained Emperor [Bảo Đại](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%E1%BA%A3o_%C4%90%E1%BA%A1i) as a figurehead.[^cite_note-79] Nationalist sentiment, intensified during World War II, helped pave the way for the communist-led Viet Minh, themselves cloaked in nationalism.[^cite_note-Guillemot_2019-80] Following the [surrender of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrender_of_Japan), they launched the [August Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_Revolution), overthrowing the [Nguyễn dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nguy%E1%BB%85n_dynasty) and seizing weapons from the Japanese. On 2 September, Ho Chi Minh [declared the independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_independence_of_the_Democratic_Republic_of_Vietnam) of the Democratic Republic of Vietnam (DRV). At the same time, following [Allied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II) decisions, British forces were deployed to Indochina to oversee the Japanese surrender south of the 16th parallel, while [Chinese Nationalist troops did so in the north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_occupation_of_northern_Vietnam,_1945%E2%80%931946). On September 23, the British supported a French coup that overthrew the DRV government in [Saigon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saigon) and reinstated French control.[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82] O.S.S. forces withdrew as the French [sought to reassert control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_southern_Vietnam_\(1945%E2%80%931946\)) in southern Indochina.

### First Indochina War

Main articles: [Civil conflicts in Vietnam (1945–1949)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_conflicts_in_Vietnam_\(1945%E2%80%931949\)), [War in southern Vietnam (1945–1946)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_southern_Vietnam_\(1945%E2%80%931946\)), and [First Indochina War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Indochina_War)



[Bảo Đại](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%E1%BA%A3o_%C4%90%E1%BA%A1i) (right) as the "supreme advisor" to the government of the [Democratic Republic of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Vietnam) led by president [Ho Chi Minh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho_Chi_Minh) (left), September 1945.

Beginning in August 1945, the Viet Minh sought to consolidate power by terrorizing and purging rival Vietnamese [nationalist groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_nationalism) and [Trotskyist activists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trotskyism_in_Vietnam).[^cite_note-Guillemot_2004-83][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKort201762–63,_81–85-84][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETran202224–30-85][^cite_note-Marr_2013-86]<sup>: 383–441 </sup> In 1946, the Franco-Chinese and [Ho–Sainteny Agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho%E2%80%93Sainteny_Agreement) facilitated a coexistence between the DRV and French that strengthened the Viet Minh while undermining the nationalists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoscha2016204–208-87][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolcombe202035,_38–44-88] That summer, the Viet Minh colluded with French forces to eliminate nationalists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKort201783–84-89][^cite_note-Guillemot_2004-83]<sup>: 205–207 </sup>[^cite_note-Reilly_2018-90]<sup>: 175–177 </sup>[^cite_note-Tran_2023-91]<sup>: 699–700 </sup>

With most of the nationalist partisans defeated,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKort201785-92][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETran202227-93] and negotiations broken down, tensions between the Viet Minh and French authorities erupted into [full-scale war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Indochina_War) in December 1946.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsselin2024a73–81-94] Surviving nationalist partisans and politico-religious groups rallied behind the exiled [Bảo Đại](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%E1%BA%A3o_%C4%90%E1%BA%A1i) to reopen negotiations with [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Fourth_Republic) to establish the [State of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Vietnam) in opposition to communist domination.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoscha2016238–241-95][^cite_note-Reilly_2018-90]<sup>: 187–188 </sup> Adhering to Marxist–Leninist principles, the Viet Minh tightened their control through a series of radical campaigns,[^cite_note-McHale_2004-96][^cite_note-Vu_2009-97] provoking disillusionment and growing defections.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETran202232–33-98]

#### Internationalization of war



Second Congress of the ICP, 1951

The [Chinese communist victory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Civil_War#Communist_southern_advances) of 1949 transformed the nature of the Indochina War.[^cite_note-Fr_IW-99]<sup>: 161 </sup> In January 1950, the [People's Republic of China](5405.md) became the first state to recognize the [Democratic Republic of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Vietnam) and persuaded the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) to do the same.[^cite_note-China_and_French-100]<sup>: 174 </sup> In line with the anticommunist [Truman Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truman_Doctrine),[^cite_note-101] the US and other [Western Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Bloc) countries recognized the French-associated [State of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Vietnam), based in Saigon, as the legitimate government.[^cite_note-McNamara-102]<sup>: 377–379 </sup>[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings201888-103] The civil war and colonial war in Indochina became internationalized and increasingly intertwined with the global [Cold War](325329.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsselin2024a93–95-104]

The outbreak of the [Korean War](16772.md) in June convinced Washington policymakers that the war in Indochina was another example of communist expansionism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings201833–35-105] Within the United States, the [Red Scare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Scare) and rise of [McCarthyism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McCarthyism) fostered public opposition to communism.[^cite_note-106] In April 1950, the Chinese leadership formed the [Chinese Military Advisory Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Military_Advisory_Group?action=edit\&redlink=1) (CMAG), marking the beginning of substantial Chinese support for the Viet Minh.[^cite_note-China_and_French-100]<sup>: 175 </sup>[^cite_note-Ang-107]<sup>: 14 </sup> Chinese weapons, expertise, and laborers transformed the Viet Minh from a guerrilla force into an army.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings201826-108][^cite_note-HistoryPlace-109] In September 1950, the US created the [Military Assistance Advisory Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Assistance_Advisory_Group) (MAAG) to screen French aid requests, advise on strategy, and train Vietnamese soldiers.[^cite_note-Herring-110]<sup>: 18 </sup> By 1954, the US had spent \$1 billion in support of the French effort, shouldering 80% of the war costs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings201835-111]

#### Battle of Dien Bien Phu

In late 1953, the CMAG and the Viet Minh planned operations in northwestern Vietnam around [Dien Bien Phu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%90i%E1%BB%87n_Bi%C3%AAn_Ph%E1%BB%A7) near the Laotian border.[^cite_note-China_and_French-100]<sup>: 182 </sup> During the [Battle of Dien Bien Phu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Dien_Bien_Phu) in 1954, Viet Minh forces encircled the French, using heavy artillery and anti-aircraft batteries to bombard the garrison.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings201849–87-112] France and the US [discussed the use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Vulture#The_plan) of [tactical nuclear weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tactical_nuclear_weapon), though how seriously this was considered is unclear.[^cite_note-Maclear-113][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings201875-114]

In May 1954, the French garrison at Dien Bien Phu surrendered. At the [Geneva Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Geneva_Conference), they negotiated a ceasefire with the Viet Minh, and the independence of Cambodia, Laos, and Vietnam was affirmed, with Vietnam placed under a temporary North–South division.[^cite_note-Geneva-115][^cite_note-116]

## Transition period

Main articles: [1954 Geneva Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Geneva_Conference), [1954 in Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_in_Vietnam), [Operation Passage to Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Passage_to_Freedom), and [Land reform in North Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_reform_in_North_Vietnam)



The [1954 Geneva Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Geneva_Conference)

Between 1953 and 1956, the DRV government instituted agrarian reforms, including rent reduction and land reform, which resulted in political oppression. Witnesses suggested about one execution per 160 village residents, totaling around 100,000 executions. As the campaign was mainly in the Red River Delta, 50,000 executions became accepted by scholars.[^cite_note-Turner-117]<sup>: 143 </sup>[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-Courtois-119]<sup>: 569 </sup>[^cite_note-120] Documents from Hungarian diplomats suggest that executions were lower,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESzalontai2005-121] though likely greater than 13,500.[^cite_note-122] The overall death toll was higher when accounting for deaths from torture, imprisonment, isolation, and suicides.[^cite_note-Holcombe_2024-123]<sup>: 356–357 </sup> In 1956, leaders in Hanoi admitted to excesses and restored much of the land to the original owners.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings201899–100-124]

At the Geneva Conference, the French and the communists came to see the division of Vietnam into two halves as a necessary solution for reaching a negotiated settlement.[^cite_note-Fr_IW-99]<sup>: 170 </sup> The Soviets and Chinese pressured Ho Chi Minh to accept the 17th parallel demarcation line, arguing that he could win the election in a couple of years.[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings201887–88-126]

The United States and the State of Vietnam objected to the division of Vietnam as a solution.[^cite_note-MRVD-127]<sup>: 37–38 </sup> The US countered with the "American Plan", with the support of the State of Vietnam and the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-PP-128]<sup>: 140 </sup> It provided for unification elections under United Nations supervision, but was rejected by the Soviets.[^cite_note-PP-128] The Diệm government called for UN-supervised elections but rejected those proposed by the Viet Minh, believing they would not be free. Neither the US, nor the State of Vietnam, signed anything at the Geneva Conference.[^cite_note-MRVD-127]<sup>: 37–38 </sup>



Caricature in a periodical criticizing the division of Vietnam by foreign powers at Geneva

The Geneva Accords of July 1954 brought a flawed peace.[^cite_note-Geneva-115] Signed between the French and the Viet Minh, the accords ended the war between French and communist forces, but it did not resolve the conflict between the two internationally recognized Vietnamese regimes, which were temporarily partitioned at the [17th parallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_Demilitarized_Zone).[^cite_note-Road-129]<sup>: 370 </sup>

Throughout the 300-day relocation period, up to one million Northerners moved South,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings201896-130][^cite_note-131] including at least 500,000 Catholics, approximately 200,000 Buddhists, and tens of thousands from ethnic minorities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoscha2016280-132] [Allen Dulles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allen_Dulles), director of the [Central Intelligence Agency](5183633.md), authorized a psychological campaign that exaggerated anti-Catholic sentiment among the Viet Minh and circulated materials falsely attributed to them.[^cite_note-Kinzer-2013-78][^cite_note-133][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings201896–97-134] The CIA's efforts played a minimal role, as Catholic migrants were driven primarily by their own convictions and circumstances rather than external psyops.[^cite_note-135]

Condemning both the communists and the French colonialists for the partition of Vietnam,[^cite_note-Displaced-136]<sup>: 45–50 </sup> [Northern émigrés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%E1%BA%AFc_54) became an important force in articulating anticommunist discourse in South Vietnam.[^cite_note-Hoang_2009-137]<sup>: 19 </sup>[^cite_note-Tran_2023-91]<sup>: 692 </sup> Meanwhile, over 100,000 Viet Minh fighters moved North for "regroupment".[^cite_note-Kolko-138]<sup>: 98 </sup> The Viet Minh left roughly 5,000 to 10,000 [cadres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadre_\(politics\)) in the South as a base for insurgency.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018104-139] The last French soldiers left South Vietnam in 1956,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018116-140] and China withdrew from North Vietnam.[^cite_note-Ang-107]<sup>: 14 </sup>



Anti-Bảo Đại revolutionists of the [State of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Vietnam) at city hall, Saigon, May 1955.

Eisenhower speculated that up to 80% of voters would have voted for Ho Chi Minh over Bao Dai in a 1954 election, emphasizing Bao Dai's shortcomings as a leader.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower1963\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsmandateforchange00eisepage372_372\]-141] The *[Pentagon Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentagon_Papers)* suggested that Diem would have fared better than Bảo Đại, with support for Ho very likely falling well below 80% by 1956.[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-MRVD-127]<sup>: 39 </sup> In 1957, independent observers from the [International Control Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Control_Commission) stated that fair elections were impossible, reporting that neither South nor North had honored the armistice agreement.[^cite_note-143]

From April to June 1955, Diệm eliminated political opposition in the south by launching operations against 'sectarian' armed groups: the [Cao Đài](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caodaism), the [Hòa Hảo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B2a_H%E1%BA%A3o), and the [organized crime](22625.md) group [Bình Xuyên](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%ACnh_Xuy%C3%AAn), which was allied with communist party secret police. The group was defeated in April following a [battle in Saigon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Saigon_\(1955\)). As broad opposition to his harsh tactics mounted, Diệm sought to blame the communists.[^cite_note-Tucker-8]

In a [referendum on the future of the State of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1955_State_of_Vietnam_referendum) in October 1955, Diệm [rigged](382165.md) the poll supervised by his brother [Ngô Đình Nhu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C3%B4_%C4%90%C3%ACnh_Nhu) and was credited with 98%, including 133% in Saigon. His American advisors had recommended a more modest winning margin of 60–70%. Diệm, however, viewed the election as a test of authority.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997224-144] He declared South Vietnam the Republic of Vietnam, with him as president.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018739-25] Likewise, Ho Chi Minh and other communists were reported to have received 99% of the vote in North Vietnamese elections.[^cite_note-Turner-117]<sup>: 193–194, 202–203, 215–217 </sup>

Political and socioeconomic discontent after land reform and Vietnam's partition led the [Vietnamese Workers' Party](400546.md) to undertake the 'correction of errors' in North Vietnam.[^cite_note-145]<sup>: 411–412, 420 </sup> Inspired by perceived reformist tendencies in Moscow and Beijing, intellectuals in Hanoi launched the unauthorized periodicals [*Nhân văn* and *Giai phẩm*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nh%C3%A2n_V%C4%83n%E2%80%93Giai_Ph%E1%BA%A9m_affair) in 1956. The Party leadership moved to suppress the movement,[^cite_note-Holcombe_2024-123]<sup>: 358–359 </sup> as it did to the [Quỳnh Lưu uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qu%E1%BB%B3nh_L%C6%B0u_uprising).[^cite_note-146]<sup>: 364 </sup>

The [domino theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domino_theory), which argued that if a country fell to communism, surrounding countries would follow, was first proposed by the [Eisenhower administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Dwight_D._Eisenhower);[^cite_note-McNamara-102]<sup>: 19 </sup> it was supported by [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy), then a [senator](24909346.md).[^cite_note-147]

## Diệm era, 1954–63

Main articles: [Ngo Dinh Diem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngo_Dinh_Diem) and [War in Vietnam (1954–1959)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Vietnam_\(1954%E2%80%931959\))

### Rule



President [Dwight D. Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower) and Secretary of State [John Foster Dulles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Foster_Dulles) greet President [Ngô Đình Diệm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngo_Dinh_Diem) of South Vietnam in Washington, 8 May 1957

By virtue of his staunch anticommunism and anticolonialism, Ngô Đình Diệm, an ardent nationalist and devout Catholic, rallied backing from people of varied regional and religious backgrounds, including many Đại Việt partisans and Hòa Hảo, Cao Đài, and Buddhist adherents.[^cite_note-Birth-148]<sup>: 324, 325 </sup> Diệm's achievement in transforming the weak South Vietnam into a centralized republic, however, did not guarantee lasting success. His [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism) tendencies and suppression of alternative views provoked resentment and opposition to his government during the communist insurgency.[^cite_note-Birth-148]<sup>: 325 </sup>

In July 1955, Diệm launched the "Denounce the Communists" campaign with the help of defectors from the Viet Minh; loosely defined and encompassing a wide range of measures under [Trần Chánh Thành](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tr%E1%BA%A7n_Ch%C3%A1nh_Th%C3%A0nh),[^cite_note-Tran_2023-91]<sup>: 689 </sup> one of its aims was to uncover and arrest communist cadres left behind in the South.[^cite_note-Road-129]<sup>: 373 </sup> Diệm instituted the death penalty in 1956 against activity deemed communist.[^cite_note-WarBegan-51] The North Vietnamese government claimed that, by November 1957, over 65,000 were imprisoned and 2,148 killed.[^cite_note-Turner-117]<sup>: 174–178 </sup> 40,000 [political prisoners](37307.md) were jailed by 1959.[^cite_note-Kolko-138]<sup>: 89 </sup> In October 1956, Diệm launched a [land reform program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_reform_in_South_Vietnam) limiting the size of rice farms per owner. 1.8m acres of farm land became available for purchase by landless people. By 1960, the process had stalled because many of Diem's biggest supporters were large landowners.[^cite_note-Collision-149]<sup>: 14–16 </sup>

In May 1957, Diệm undertook a [10-day state visit to the US](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C3%B4_%C4%90%C3%ACnh_Di%E1%BB%87m_presidential_visit_to_the_United_States). Eisenhower pledged his continued support, and a parade was held in Diệm's honor. But Secretary of State Dulles privately conceded Diệm had to be backed because they could find no better alternative.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997230-150]

### Insurgency in the South, 1954–60

Main articles: [Viet Cong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viet_Cong) and [War in Vietnam (1959–1963)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Vietnam_\(1959%E2%80%931963\))

At least 50,000 Communist Party members and soldiers with hidden weapon caches were deliberately left in the South following the Geneva Accords in 1954.[^cite_note-Road-129]<sup>: 371 </sup> Between 1954 and 1957, the Diệm government succeeded in preventing large-scale unrest in the countryside. In April 1957, insurgents launched an assassination campaign, referred to as "extermination of traitors".[^cite_note-McNamera35-151] 17 people were killed in the [Châu Đốc massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ch%C3%A2u_%C4%90%E1%BB%91c_massacre) at a bar in July.[^cite_note-WarBegan-51] By early 1959, Diệm had come to regard the violence as an organized campaign and implemented Law 10/59, which made political violence punishable by death.[^cite_note-152] There had been division among former Viet Minh, whose main goal was to hold elections promised in the Geneva Accords, leading to activities separate from the other communists and anti-GVN (Government of the Republic of Vietnam) activists. [Douglas Pike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Pike) estimated that insurgents carried out 2,000 abductions, and 1,700 assassinations of officials, village chiefs, hospital workers and teachers from 1957 to 1960.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018106-153][^cite_note-WarBegan-51] Violence between insurgents and government forces increased from 180 clashes in January 1960, to 545 in September.[^cite_note-cmh-154]

In September 1960, [COSVN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Office_for_South_Vietnam), North Vietnam's southern headquarters, ordered a coordinated uprising in South Vietnam and a third of the population was soon living in areas of communist control.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018106–107-155] In December, North Vietnam formally created the [Viet Cong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viet_Cong) (VC) with the intent of uniting all anti-GVN insurgents, including non-communists. It was formed in [Memot, Cambodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memot_District), and directed through COSVN.[^cite_note-Ang-107]<sup>: 55–58 </sup> The VC placed "emphasis on the withdrawal of American advisors and influence, on land reform and liberalization of the GVN, on [coalition government](6036.md) and the neutralization of Vietnam." Identities of the leaders were often kept secret.[^cite_note-WarBegan-51]

#### North Vietnamese involvement

See also: [North Vietnamese invasion of Laos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Vietnamese_invasion_of_Laos) and [Ho Chi Minh trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho_Chi_Minh_trail)

In March 1956, southern communist leader [Lê Duẩn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%AA_Du%E1%BA%A9n) presented a plan to revive the insurgency entitled "The Road to the South", to the Politburo in Hanoi. However, as China and the Soviets opposed confrontation, his plan was rejected.[^cite_note-Ang-107]<sup>: 58 </sup> Despite this, the North Vietnamese leadership approved tentative measures to revive southern insurgency in December.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOlsonRoberts200867-50] Communist forces were under a single command structure set up in 1958.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMilitary_History_Institute_of_Vietnam200268-156] In May 1958, North Vietnamese forces seized the transportation hub at [Tchepone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tchepone) in Southern Laos near the demilitarized zone, between North and South Vietnam.[^cite_note-Prados-1999-157]<sup>: 24 </sup>

The North Vietnamese Communist Party approved a "people's war" on the South in January 1959,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018119–120-158] and, in May, [Group 559](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_559) was established to upgrade the [Ho Chi Minh trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho_Chi_Minh_trail), then a six-month mountain trek through Laos. On 28 July, North Vietnamese and [Pathet Lao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pathet_Lao) forces invaded Laos, fighting the [Royal Lao Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Lao_Army) along the border.[^cite_note-Morrocco-1985-159]<sup>: 26 </sup> About 500 of the "regroupees" of 1954 were sent south on the trail during its first year of operation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMilitary_History_Institute_of_Vietnam2002xi-160] The first arms delivery was completed in August 1959.[^cite_note-161] At the [Third Party Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3rd_National_Congress_of_the_Workers'_Party_of_Vietnam) in September 1960, the leaders of the [Workers' Party of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers'_Party_of_Vietnam) authorised the creation of the [National Liberation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viet_Cong) while concealing their control over the organisation.[^cite_note-Insurgency-162]<sup>: 428–429 </sup>

## Kennedy's escalation, 1961–63

Main articles: [War in Vietnam (1959–1963)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Vietnam_\(1959%E2%80%931963\)) and [Strategic Hamlet Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Hamlet_Program)



President Kennedy's news conference of 23 March 1961

In the [1960 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_United_States_presidential_election), John F. Kennedy defeated Richard Nixon. Although Eisenhower warned Kennedy about Laos and Vietnam, Europe and Latin America "loomed larger than Asia on his sights."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997264-163]

Kennedy remained committed to the Cold War foreign policy inherited from the Truman and Eisenhower administrations. In 1961, the US had 50,000 troops based in South Korea, and Kennedy faced four crisis situations: the failure of the [Bay of Pigs Invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_of_Pigs_Invasion) he had approved in April,[^cite_note-164] settlement negotiations between the pro-Western government of Laos and the Pathet Lao communist movement in May,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997265-165] construction of the [Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Wall) in August, and the Cuban Missile Crisis in October. Kennedy believed another failure to stop communist expansion would irreparably damage US credibility. He was determined to "draw a line in the sand" and prevent a communist victory in Vietnam. He told *[The New York Times](30680.md)* after the Vienna summit with Khrushchev, "Now we have a problem making our power credible and Vietnam looks like the place."[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167]

Kennedy's policy toward South Vietnam assumed Diệm and his forces had to defeat the guerrillas on their own. He was against the deployment of American combat troops and observed "to introduce U.S. forces in large numbers there today, while it might have an initially favorable military impact, would almost certainly lead to adverse political and, in the long run, adverse military consequences."[^cite_note-168] The quality of the South Vietnamese military, however, remained poor. Poor leadership, corruption, and political promotions weakened the ARVN. The frequency of guerrilla attacks rose as the insurgency gathered steam. While Hanoi's support for the VC played a role, South Vietnamese governmental incompetence was at the core of the crisis.[^cite_note-McNamara-102]<sup>: 369 </sup>



President Kennedy meeting with Secretary of Defense [McNamara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_McNamara), in June 1962

Kennedy advisors [Maxwell Taylor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxwell_D._Taylor) and [Walt Rostow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Rostow) [recommended](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taylor-Rostow_Report) US troops be sent to South Vietnam disguised as flood relief workers.[^cite_note-169] Kennedy rejected the idea but increased military assistance. In April 1962, [John Kenneth Galbraith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kenneth_Galbraith) warned Kennedy of the "danger we shall replace the French as a colonial force...and bleed as the French did."[^cite_note-170] Eisenhower put 900 advisors in Vietnam, and by November 1963, Kennedy had put 16,000.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018131-171]

The [Strategic Hamlet Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Hamlet_Program) was initiated in late 1961. This joint US–South Vietnamese program attempted to resettle the rural population into fortified villages. It was implemented in early 1962 and involved forced relocation and segregation of rural South Vietnamese, into new communities where the peasantry would be isolated from the VC. It was hoped these new communities would provide security for the peasants and strengthen the tie between them and central government. However, by November 1963 the program had waned, and it ended in 1964.[^cite_note-Tucker-8]<sup>: 1070 </sup> In July 1962, 14 nations, including China, South Vietnam, the Soviet Union, North Vietnam, and the US, signed the [International Agreement on the Neutrality of Laos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Agreement_on_the_Neutrality_of_Laos).

### Ousting and assassination of Diệm

Main articles: [1963 South Vietnamese coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_South_Vietnamese_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) and [Arrest and assassination of Ngo Dinh Diem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrest_and_assassination_of_Ngo_Dinh_Diem)

See also: [United States in the Vietnam War](8453309.md), [Krulak–Mendenhall mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krulak%E2%80%93Mendenhall_mission), and [McNamara–Taylor mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McNamara%E2%80%93Taylor_mission)

The inept performance of the ARVN was exemplified by failed actions such as the [Battle of Ap Bac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ap_Bac) in January 1963, in which the VC won a battle against a much larger and better-equipped South Vietnamese force, many of whose officers seemed reluctant to engage.[^cite_note-Sheehan-172]<sup>: 201–206 </sup> The ARVN lost 83 soldiers and 5 US helicopters, serving to ferry troops shot down by VC forces, while the VC lost only 18 soldiers. The ARVN forces were led by Diệm's most trusted general, [Huỳnh Văn Cao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu%E1%BB%B3nh_V%C4%83n_Cao). Cao was a Catholic, promoted due to religion and fidelity rather than skill, and his main role was to preserve his forces to stave off coups. Washington policymakers concluded Diệm was incapable of defeating the communists and might even make a deal with Ho Chi Minh. He seemed concerned only with fending off coups and had become paranoid after attempts in [1960](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_South_Vietnamese_coup_attempt) and [1962](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1962_South_Vietnamese_Independence_Palace_bombing), which he partly attributed to US encouragement. [Robert F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Kennedy) noted, "Diệm wouldn't make even the slightest concessions. He was difficult to reason with ..."[^cite_note-173]

Diệm's alienation of allies who had previously supported his anticommunist efforts, including Buddhist leaders, American officials, and military generals, fueled the unfolding 1963 crisis.[^cite_note-Crisis-174]<sup>: 473 </sup> Discontent with Diệm's policies exploded in May 1963, following the [Huế Phật Đản shootings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu%E1%BA%BF_Ph%E1%BA%ADt_%C4%90%E1%BA%A3n_shootings) of 9 Buddhists protesting the ban on displaying the [Buddhist flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_flag) on [Vesak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vesak). This resulted in mass protests—the [Buddhist crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_crisis)—against policies perceived as favoring Catholics over Buddhists. [Ngô Đình Thục](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C3%B4_%C4%90%C3%ACnh_Th%E1%BB%A5c), Diệm's elder brother and the Archbishop of Huế, held his anniversary celebrations shortly before Vesak; they were bankrolled by the government, and Catholic flags were prominently displayed. Diệm refused to make concessions to the Buddhists or take responsibility for the deaths. In August 1963, the [ARVN Special Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_of_the_Republic_of_Vietnam_Special_Forces) of Colonel [Lê Quang Tung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%AA_Quang_Tung), loyal to Diệm's brother Ngô Đình Nhu, [raided pagodas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X%C3%A1_L%E1%BB%A3i_Pagoda_raids), causing destruction and hundreds of deaths. The Republic of Vietnam sought to harness religious nationalism by promoting spiritual values in opposition to communism's atheism; however, this approach inadvertently amplified religious consciousness that challenged the state's authority.[^cite_note-NPV_2018-175]



ARVN forces capture a Viet Cong

US officials began discussing [regime change](154074.md) during 1963. The [United States Department of State](31975.md) wanted to encourage a coup, while the Pentagon favored Diệm. Chief among the proposed changes was removal of Diệm's brother Nhu, who controlled the secret police and special forces, and was seen as being behind Buddhist repression and the Ngô family's rule. This proposal was conveyed to the US embassy in Saigon in [Cable 243](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cable_243). The CIA contacted generals planning to remove Diệm, and told them the US would not oppose this, nor punish them by cutting off aid. Diệm was overthrown and executed, along with his brother, on 2 November 1963. When Kennedy was informed, Maxwell Taylor remembered he "rushed from the room with a look of shock and dismay on his face."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997326-176] Kennedy had not anticipated Diệm's murder. The US ambassador, [Henry Cabot Lodge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Cabot_Lodge_Jr.), invited coup leaders to the embassy and congratulated them. Lodge informed Kennedy that "the prospects now are for a shorter war".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997327-177] Kennedy wrote to Lodge congratulating him for "a fine job".[^cite_note-178]

Following the coup, chaos ensued. Hanoi took advantage and increased its support for the VC. South Vietnam entered political instability, as one military government toppled another. Each new regime was viewed by the communists as a puppet of the Americans; whatever the failings of Diệm, his credentials as a nationalist had been impeccable.[^cite_note-McNamara-102]<sup>: 328 </sup> US advisors were embedded in the South Vietnamese armed forces. They were criticized for ignoring the political nature of the insurgency.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDemma1989-179] The Kennedy administration sought to refocus US efforts on pacification – which in this case was defined as countering the insurgency[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181] – and ["winning the hearts and minds"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hearts_and_Minds_\(Vietnam_War\)) of the population. Military leadership in Washington, however, was hostile to any role for US advisors other than troop training.[^cite_note-182] General [Paul Harkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_D._Harkins), the [commander of US forces in South Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COMUSMACV), confidently predicted victory by Christmas 1963.[^cite_note-Herring-110]<sup>: 103 </sup> The CIA was less optimistic, warning that "the Viet Cong by and large retain de facto control of much of the countryside and have steadily increased the overall intensity of the effort".[^cite_note-183]

Paramilitary officers from the CIA's [Special Activities Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Activities_Division) trained and led [Hmong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hmong_people) tribesmen in Laos and into Vietnam. The indigenous forces were in the tens of thousands and conducted direct action missions, led by paramilitary officers, against the Communist Pathet Lao forces and their North Vietnamese supporters.[^cite_note-184] The CIA participated in the [Military Assistance Command, Vietnam – Studies and Observations Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Assistance_Command,_Vietnam_%E2%80%93_Studies_and_Observations_Group) (MAC-V SOG).[^cite_note-185]

### Hanoi's road to escalation

Within the [Politburo of the Workers' Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politburo_of_the_Communist_Party_of_Vietnam), since 1960, [Lê Duẩn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%AA_Du%E1%BA%A9n) and [Nguyễn Chí Thanh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nguy%E1%BB%85n_Ch%C3%AD_Thanh) had opposed [Khrushchev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev)'s policy of [peaceful coexistence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peaceful_coexistence).[^cite_note-Road-129]<sup>: 385 </sup> In 1963, Hanoi formally rejected peaceful coexistence as a strategic principle.[^cite_note-Crisis-174]<sup>: 474 </sup> The opening of the Ho Chi Minh Sea Trail in late 1962, distinct from [the land trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho_Chi_Minh_trail) through Laos, together with significant increases in the size of the [Viet Cong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viet_Cong) armed forces, gave communist military commanders the capacity to wage a much larger and deadlier campaign in the South. At the same time, the intensification of the [Sino-Soviet split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Soviet_split) during 1963 enabled [North Vietnamese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Vietnam) leaders to obtain Chinese assurances of support for a more aggressive war approach.[^cite_note-Crisis-174]<sup>: 474 </sup> In April 1960, North Vietnam imposed military conscription for men. About 40,000 communist soldiers infiltrated the South between 1961 and 1963.[^cite_note-Ang-107]<sup>: 76 </sup> [Denouncing revisionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-revisionism) Soviet policies, Beijing increasingly endorsed Hanoi's war efforts in South Vietnam.[^cite_note-Road-129]<sup>: 385 </sup>

## Gulf of Tonkin and Johnson's escalation, 1963–69

Main article: [Joint warfare in South Vietnam, 1963–1969](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_warfare_in_South_Vietnam,_1963%E2%80%931969)

Further information: [United States in the Vietnam War § Americanization](8453309.md#Americanization), [January 1964 South Vietnamese coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_1964_South_Vietnamese_coup), [September 1964 South Vietnamese coup attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_1964_South_Vietnamese_coup_attempt), [December 1964 South Vietnamese coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/December_1964_South_Vietnamese_coup), and [1965 South Vietnamese coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1965_South_Vietnamese_coup)

Kennedy [was assassinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_John_F._Kennedy) on 22 November 1963. Vice President [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) had not been heavily involved with policy toward Vietnam;[^cite_note-Karnow_1997_336_339-186][^cite_note-188] however he immediately focused on it.[^cite_note-189] Johnson knew he had inherited a deteriorating situation,[^cite_note-Karnow_1997_339-190] but adhered to the widely accepted domino argument for defending the South: Should they retreat or appease, either action would imperil other nations.[^cite_note-191] Findings from RAND's [Viet Cong Motivation and Morale Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viet_Cong_Motivation_and_Morale_Project) bolstered his confidence that an air war would weaken the insurgency. Some argue the policy of North Vietnam was not to topple other non-communist governments in South East Asia.[^cite_note-McNamara-102]<sup>: 48 </sup>

The military revolutionary council, meeting in lieu of a strong South Vietnamese leader, had 12 members. It was headed by General [Dương Văn Minh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C6%B0%C6%A1ng_V%C4%83n_Minh), whom journalist [Stanley Karnow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Karnow), recalled as "a model of lethargy".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997340-192] Lodge cabled home about Minh: "Will he be strong enough to get on top of things?" Minh's regime was overthrown in January 1964 by General [Nguyễn Khánh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nguy%E1%BB%85n_Kh%C3%A1nh).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997341-193] There was persistent instability in the military: several coups—not all successful—occurred in a short period of time.

### Gulf of Tonkin incident

Main article: [Gulf of Tonkin incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Tonkin_incident)

Further information: [Credibility gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credibility_gap)



[Republic F-105D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_F-105_Thunderchief) fighter-bombers of the US Air Force drop [750 pound bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M117_bomb) during [Operation Rolling Thunder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Rolling_Thunder)

In early 1964, South Vietnamese commandos began launching US-backed raids on the North Vietnamese coast as a part of [Operation 34A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_34A).[^cite_note-\:0-194] On 2 August 1964, [USS *Maddox*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Maddox_\(DD-731\)), conducting a [DESOTO patrol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DESOTO_patrol) to support the commandos, fired upon and damaged torpedo boats approaching it in the Gulf of Tonkin.[^cite_note-Kolko-138]<sup>: 124 </sup> A second attack was reported two days later on [USS *Turner Joy*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Turner_Joy) and *Maddox*. A document declassified in 2005 revealed there was no attack on 4 August, and that the NSA had skewed intelligence to create the impression that an attack had occurred.[^cite_note-195] The circumstances of the incident were murky at the time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018218–219-196] Johnson commented to Undersecretary of State George Ball that "those sailors out there may have been shooting at flying fish."[^cite_note-197] Secretary of Defense MacNamara misled the public and Congress by concealing his knowledge of the raids which had provoked the attack on 2 August.[^cite_note-\:0-194] The incident led the [US to strike Vietnamese gunboats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Pierce_Arrow), and prompted Congress to approve the [Gulf of Tonkin Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Tonkin_Resolution) on 7 August.[^cite_note-198]<sup>: 222–244 </sup> This granted the president power "to take all necessary measures to repel any armed attack against the forces of the United States and to prevent further aggression" and Johnson relied on this as giving him authority to expand the war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018221-199] Johnson pledged he was not "committing American boys to fighting a war that I think ought to be fought by the boys of Asia to help protect their own land".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018227-200]

The [National Security Council](44026.md) recommended an escalation of the bombing of North Vietnam. Following an [attack on a US Army base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_on_Camp_Holloway) in February 1965,[^cite_note-201] airstrikes were initiated, while Soviet Premier [Alexei Kosygin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexei_Kosygin) was on a [state visit](327283.md) to North Vietnam. [Operation Rolling Thunder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Rolling_Thunder) and [Operation Arc Light](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Arc_Light) expanded aerial bombardment and ground support operations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENalty199897,_261-202] The bombing campaign, which lasted three years, was intended to force North Vietnam to cease support for the VC by threatening to destroy North Vietnamese air defenses and infrastructure. It was additionally aimed at bolstering South Vietnamese morale.[^cite_note-203] Between 1965 and 1968, *Rolling Thunder* deluged the north with a million tons of missiles, rockets and bombs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997468-204]

### Bombing of Laos

The US began sustained bombing of the [Ho Chi Minh trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho_Chi_Minh_trail) to disrupt communist supply lines. With Lao Prime Minister [Souvanna Phouma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Souvanna_Phouma)'s consent, US airstrikes began in December 1964 and escalated into a continuous bombing campaign by April 1965 that lasted for over three years.[^cite_note-205]<sup>: 409 </sup>

Aerial bombardment against the Pathet Lao and PAVN forces was undertaken by the US to prevent the collapse of the government, and deny use of the Ho Chi Minh Trail. Between 1964 and 1973, the US dropped two million tons of bombs on Laos, similar to the 2.1 million tons of bombs it dropped on Europe and Asia during World War II, making Laos the most heavily bombed country in history.[^cite_note-KiernanTaylor-206]

The objective of stopping North Vietnam and the VC was never reached. The [Chief of Staff of the United States Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_Staff_of_the_United_States_Air_Force), [Curtis LeMay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curtis_LeMay), however, had long advocated saturation bombing in Vietnam and wrote of the communists that "we're going to bomb them back into the Stone Age".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018328-207]

### The 1964 offensive



ARVN Forces and a US Advisor inspect a downed helicopter, [Battle of Dong Xoai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Dong_Xoai), June 1965

Following the Tonkin Resolution, Hanoi anticipated the arrival of US troops and expanded the VC, as well as sending PAVN personnel southwards. They were outfitting the VC forces and standardizing their equipment with [AK-47](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AK-47) rifles and other supplies, as well as forming the [9th Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9th_Division_\(Vietnam\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018223-208][^cite_note-209] "From a strength of approximately 5,000 at the start of 1959 the Viet Cong's ranks grew to about 100,000 at the end of 1964 ... Between 1961 and 1964 the Army's strength rose from about 850,000 to nearly a million men."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDemma1989-179] US troop numbers deployed were much lower: 2,000 in 1961, rising to 16,500 in 1964.[^cite_note-Kahin-210] The use of captured equipment decreased, while more ammunition and supplies were required to maintain regular units. Group 559 was tasked with expanding the Ho Chi Minh Trail, in light of US bombardment. The war had shifted into the final, conventional phase of Hanoi's [three-stage protracted warfare model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viet_Cong_and_PAVN_strategy,_organization_and_structure#The_Protracted_War_conflict_model). The VC was tasked with destroying the ARVN, and capturing and holding areas; however, it was not yet strong enough to assault towns and cities.

In December 1964, ARVN forces suffered losses at the [Battle of Bình Giã](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_B%C3%ACnh_Gi%C3%A3),[^cite_note-211] in a battle both sides viewed as a watershed. Previously, the VC had utilized hit-and-run guerrilla tactics. At Binh Gia, they defeated a strong ARVN force in a conventional battle and remained in the field for four days.[^cite_note-McNeill-212]<sup>: 58 </sup> Tellingly, South Vietnamese forces were again defeated in June 1965 at the [Battle of Đồng Xoài](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_%C4%90%E1%BB%93ng_Xo%C3%A0i).[^cite_note-McNeill-212]<sup>: 94 </sup>

### American ground war

See also: [Buddhist Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_Uprising)



A Marine from [1st Battalion, 3rd Marines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_Battalion,_3rd_Marines), moves a suspected Viet Cong during a search and clear operation held by the battalion 15 miles (24 km) west of [Da Nang Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da_Nang_Air_Base), 1965.

On 8 March 1965, 3,500 [US Marines](17349325.md) were landed near [Da Nang](425140.md), South Vietnam.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018246–247-213] This marked the beginning of America's ground war. US public opinion overwhelmingly supported the deployment.[^cite_note-214] The Marines' initial assignment was defense of [Da Nang Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da_Nang_Air_Base). The first deployment was increased to nearly 200,000 by December.[^cite_note-McNamara-102]<sup>: 349–351 </sup> US military had been schooled in offensive warfare. Regardless of policy, US commanders were institutionally and psychologically unsuited to defensive missions.[^cite_note-McNamara-102]<sup>: 349–351 </sup>

General [William Westmoreland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Westmoreland) informed Admiral [U. S. Grant Sharp Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U._S._Grant_Sharp_Jr.), commander of US Pacific forces, that the situation was critical,[^cite_note-McNamara-102]<sup>: 349–351 </sup> "I am convinced that U.S. troops with their energy, mobility, and firepower can successfully take the fight to the NLF (Viet Cong)".[^cite_note-215] With this recommendation, Westmoreland advocated an aggressive departure from America's defensive posture and sidelining of the South Vietnamese. By ignoring ARVN units, the US commitment became open-ended.[^cite_note-McNamara-102]<sup>: 353 </sup> Westmoreland outlined a three-point plan to win:

- Phase 1. Commitment of US and allied forces necessary to halt the losing trend by the end of 1965.

- Phase 2. US and allied forces mount major offensive actions to seize the initiative to destroy guerrilla and organized enemy forces. This phase would end when the enemy had been worn down and driven back from major populated areas.

- Phase 3. If the enemy persisted, a period of 12–18 months following Phase 2 would be required for final destruction of forces remaining in remote base areas.

  [^cite_note-216]

The plan was approved by Johnson and marked a profound departure from the insistence that South Vietnam was responsible for defeating the VC. Westmoreland predicted victory by December 1967.[^cite_note-217] Johnson did not communicate this change to the media; instead, he emphasized continuity.[^cite_note-218] The change in policy depended on matching the North Vietnamese and VC in a contest of [attrition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attrition_warfare) and [morale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morale). The opponents were locked in an [escalation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conflict_escalation) cycle.[^cite_note-McNamara-102]<sup>: 353–354 </sup> However, Johnson ruled out invasion of North Vietnam due to fears of Chinese or Soviet intervention.[^cite_note-Lind1999-219]<sup>: 83–89 </sup> Westmoreland and McNamara touted the [body count](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body_count#Vietnam_War) system for gauging victory, a metric that proved flawed.[^cite_note-Mohr-220]



Peasants suspected of being Viet Cong under detention of US Army, 1966

The American buildup transformed the South Vietnamese economy and profoundly affected society. South Vietnam was inundated with manufactured goods. Washington encouraged its [SEATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia_Treaty_Organization) allies to contribute troops; Australia, New Zealand, Thailand and the Philippines[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997556-221] agreed. South Korea asked to join the [Many Flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Many_Flags) program in return for economic compensation. Major allies, however, notably Canada and the UK, declined troop requests.[^cite_note-222]

The US and allies mounted complex [search and destroy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Search_and_destroy) operations. In November 1965, the US engaged in its first major battle with the PAVN, the [Battle of Ia Drang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ia_Drang).[^cite_note-223] The operation was the first large scale helicopter air assault by the US, and first to employ [Boeing B-52 Stratofortress](18933037.md) bombers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018284–285-224] These tactics continued in 1966–67; however, the PAVN/VC insurgents remained elusive and demonstrated tactical flexibility. By 1967, the war had generated large-scale internal refugees, 2 million in South Vietnam, with 125,000 people evacuated and rendered homeless during [Operation Masher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Masher) alone,[^cite_note-225] the largest search and destroy operation to that point. Operation Masher had negligible impact however, as the PAVN/VC returned to the province just four months afterwards.[^cite_note-Ward-226]<sup>: 153–156 </sup> Despite major operations, which the VC and PAVN would evade, the war was characterized by smaller-unit engagements.[^cite_note-GS-227] The VC and PAVN would initiate 90% of large firefights, and thus the PAVN/VC would retain strategic initiative despite overwhelming US force and firepower deployment.[^cite_note-GS-227] The PAVN and Viet Cong had developed strategies capable of countering US doctrines and tactics: see [NLF and PAVN battle tactics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NLF_and_PAVN_battle_tactics).

Meanwhile, South Vietnamese politics began to stabilize with the arrival of prime minister Air Marshal [Nguyễn Cao Kỳ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nguy%E1%BB%85n_Cao_K%E1%BB%B3) and figurehead chief of state, General [Nguyễn Văn Thiệu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nguy%E1%BB%85n_V%C4%83n_Thi%E1%BB%87u), in mid-1965. In 1967, Thieu became president with Ky as his deputy, after rigged elections.[^cite_note-229] Though nominally a civilian government, Kỳ was supposed to maintain real power through a behind-the-scenes military body. However, Thiệu outmanoeuvred and sidelined Kỳ. Thiệu was accused of murdering Kỳ loyalists through contrived military accidents. Thiệu remained president until 1975, having won a [one-candidate election in 1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_South_Vietnamese_presidential_election).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997706-230]

Johnson employed a "policy of minimum candor"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow199718-231] with the media. Military information officers sought to manage coverage by emphasizing stories that portrayed progress. This damaged public trust in official pronouncements. As coverage of the war and the Pentagon diverged, a [credibility gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credibility_gap) developed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow199718-231] Despite Johnson and Westmoreland proclaiming victory and Westmoreland stating the "end is coming into view",[^cite_note-232] internal reports in the *[Pentagon Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentagon_Papers)* indicate VC forces retained strategic initiative and controlled their losses. VC attacks against static US positions accounted for 30% of engagements, VC/PAVN ambushes and encirclements for 23%, American ambushes against VC/PAVN forces for 9%, and American forces attacking VC emplacements only 5%.[^cite_note-GS-227]

## Tet Offensive and its aftermath

Main articles: [Tet Offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tet_Offensive) and [United States news media and the Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_news_media_and_the_Vietnam_War)



Viet Cong before departing to participate in the Tet Offensive around Saigon-Gia Dinh



ARVN forces assault a stronghold in the [Mekong Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mekong_Delta)

In late 1967, the PAVN lured American forces into the hinterlands at [Đắk Tô](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Dak_To) and at the Marine [Khe Sanh combat base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Khe_Sanh), where the US fought [The Hill Fights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hill_Fights). These were part of a diversionary strategy to draw US forces towards the Central Highlands.[^cite_note-233] Preparations were underway for the [Tet Offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tet_Offensive), with the intention of [Văn Tiến Dũng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C4%83n_Ti%E1%BA%BFn_D%C5%A9ng) forces to launch "direct attacks on the American and puppet nerve centers—Saigon, [Huế](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu%E1%BA%BF), Danang, all the cities, towns and main bases \[...]".[^cite_note-Wilson-234] Le Duan sought to placate critics of the stalemate by a decisive victory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENguyen201290–94-235] He reasoned this could be achieved through sparking an uprising in the towns and cities,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENguyen2012148-236] along with defections among ARVN units, who were on leave during the truce period.[^cite_note-237]

The Tet Offensive began in January 1968, as over 100 cities were attacked by over 85,000 VC/PAVN troops, including assaults on military installations, headquarters, and government buildings, including the [US Embassy in Saigon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tet_offensive_attack_on_the_United_States_embassy).[^cite_note-McNamara-102]<sup>: 363–365 </sup> US and South Vietnamese forces were shocked by the scale, intensity and deliberative planning, as the infiltration of Communist personnel and weapons into the cities was accomplished covertly.[^cite_note-Wilson-234] The offensive constituted an [intelligence failure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligence_failure) on the scale of [Pearl Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_on_Pearl_Harbor).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997556-221] Most cities were recaptured within weeks, [except the former imperial capital Huế](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Hu%E1%BA%BF), which PAVN/VC troops held on for 26 days.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBowden2017495-238] They [executed approximately 2,800 unarmed Huế](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre_at_Hu%E1%BA%BF) civilians, ARVN POWs, and foreigners they considered spies.[^cite_note-239][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBowden2017495-238] In the Battle of Huế American forces employed massive firepower that left 80% of the city in ruins.[^cite_note-Kolko-138]<sup>: 308–309 </sup> At [Quảng Trị City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Quang_Tri_\(1968\)), the [ARVN Airborne Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Vietnam_Airborne_Division), the 1st Division and a US 1st Cavalry Division regiment held out and overcame an assault intended to capture the city.[^cite_note-Villard-240][^cite_note-Ankony-241]<sup>: 104 </sup> In Saigon, VC/PAVN fighters had captured areas in and around the city, attacking key installations before US and ARVN forces dislodged them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018479-242] [Peter Arnett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Arnett) reported an infantry commander saying of the [Battle of Bến Tre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_B%E1%BA%BFn_Tre) that "it became necessary to destroy the village in order to save it".[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244]



The ruins of a section of Saigon, in the Cholon neighborhood, following fierce fighting between ARVN forces and Viet Cong Main Force battalions

During the first month of Tet, 1,100 American and allied troops, 2,100 ARVN and 14,000 civilians were killed.[^cite_note-Trieu-245] After two months, 5,000 ARVN and 4,000 US forces had been killed and 46,000 wounded.[^cite_note-Trieu-245] The US claimed 17,000 PAVN/VC had been killed and 15,000 wounded.[^cite_note-Ankony-241]<sup>: 104 </sup>[^cite_note-Villard-240]<sup>: 82 </sup> A month later the [May Offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_Offensive) was launched; it demonstrated the VC were still capable of orchestrating nationwide offensives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018488–489-246] Two months later the [Phase III offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phase_III_offensive) was launched. Communist losses across the offensives was 45,267 killed and 111,179 total casualties, mostly Viet Cong.[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248] It had become the bloodiest year up to then. The failure to spark a general uprising and lack of defections among the ARVN units meant Hanoi's goals had failed at enormous cost.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENguyen2012148–149-249]

Prior to Tet, in November 1967, Westmoreland had spearheaded a public relations drive for the Johnson administration to bolster flagging public support.[^cite_note-Witz-250] In a speech to the [National Press Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Press_Club_\(United_States\)) he said "the end comes into view".[^cite_note-251] Thus, the public was shocked and confused by the Tet Offensive.[^cite_note-Witz-250] Public approval of his performance dropped from 48% to 36%, and endorsement for the war fell from 40% to 26%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997546-252] The public turned against Johnson as the offensives contradicted claims of progress.[^cite_note-Witz-250]

During 1968, Westmoreland considered the use of [nuclear weapons](21785.md) in a contingency plan codenamed [Fracture Jaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fracture_Jaw), which was abandoned when it became known to the White House.[^cite_note-253] Westmoreland and Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff General [Earle Wheeler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earle_Wheeler) requested more than 200,000 additional troops.[^cite_note-254] This quickly leaked to the media, and the fallout combined with intelligence failures caused Westmoreland to be removed in March 1968, succeeded by his deputy [Creighton Abrams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creighton_Abrams).[^cite_note-255]

On 10 May 1968, [peace talks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Peace_Accords) began between the US and North Vietnam in Paris. Negotiations stagnated for five months, until Johnson halted the bombing of North Vietnam. Hanoi realized it could not achieve a "total victory" and employed a strategy known as "talking while fighting, fighting while talking", in which offensives occurred concurrently with negotiations.[^cite_note-256]

Johnson declined to run for re-election as his approval rating slumped from 48% to 36%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018486-257] His escalation of the war divided Americans, cost 30,000 American lives by that point and is regarded as having destroyed his presidency.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018486-257] Robert McNamara later said "the dangerous illusion of victory by the United States was therefore dead",[^cite_note-McNamara-102]<sup>: 367 </sup> but this was an exaggeration. There continued to be important Americans who hoped for victory, and Johnson was among them. In the speech of March 31, 1968, in which he announced he would not run for re-election, he offered to begin peace negotiations with Hanoi. He said that in an effort to get negotiations started, he was "reducing—substantially reducing—the present level of hostilities."[^cite_note-lbjspeech-258] This was misleading. He was increasing the military pressure on enemy forces, trying to ensure that if the negotiations produced an agreement, it would be one representing a defeat for Hanoi.

Johnson did not make the huge increase in US troop strength that Generals Westmoreland and Wheeler had requested a few weeks earlier, but he did send significant reinforcements. There were about 515,000 US military personnel in Vietnam at the time of his speech. There were 536,000 two months later.[^cite_note-259] He narrowed the area of North Vietnam on which US bombs fell, but in April 1968, the first month in which bombing was limited to the southern part of North Vietnam, the United States dropped almost twice as many tons of bombs there as had been dropped on the whole of North Vietnam in March. More importantly, US bombing of Indochina as a whole was increasing. The most the US had dropped in Indochina in any month before Johnson's speech had been about 98,000 tons. In April 1968, the month after his speech, the figure rose to almost 113,000 tons.[^cite_note-260]

Vietnam was a major issue during the [United States presidential election in 1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_United_States_presidential_election). The election was won by Republican Richard Nixon who claimed to have a secret plan to end the war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018515-261][^cite_note-262]

## Vietnamization, 1969–1972



A [chiêu hồi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chieu_Hoi) ('open arms') center for defectors from communist forces to the [Republic of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Vietnam), 1969.

### Nuclear threats and diplomacy

Nixon began troop withdrawals in 1969. His plan to build up the ARVN so it could take over defense of South Vietnam became known as "[Vietnamization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamization)". As the PAVN/VC recovered from their 1968 losses and avoided contact, Abrams conducted operations aimed at disrupting logistics, with better use of firepower and more cooperation with the ARVN.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018517-263] In October 1969, Nixon had ordered B-52s with nuclear weapons [to race to the border of Soviet airspace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Giant_Lance) to convince the Soviets, in accordance with the [madman theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madman_theory), he was capable of anything to end the war.[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265] Nixon had sought *[détente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A9tente)* with the Soviet Union and [rapprochement with China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-American_relations#Rapprochement), which decreased tensions and led to nuclear arms reductions. However, the Soviets continued to supply North Vietnam.[^cite_note-266][^cite_note-267]

### Hanoi's war strategy

On 2 September 1969, Ho Chi Minh died.[^cite_note-268] The failure of the Tet Offensive to spark an uprising in the south caused a shift in Hanoi's war strategy, and the [Giáp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%B5_Nguy%C3%AAn_Gi%C3%A1p)-[Chinh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tr%C6%B0%E1%BB%9Dng_Chinh) "Northern-First" faction regained control over military affairs from the Lê Duẩn-[Hoàng Văn Thái](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho%C3%A0ng_V%C4%83n_Th%C3%A1i) "Southern-First" faction.[^cite_note-Currey-269]<sup>: 272–274 </sup> An unconventional victory was sidelined in favor of conventional through conquest.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENguyen2012196–205-270] Large-scale offensives were rolled back in favor of [small-unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_intensity_conflict) and [sapper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sapper#PAVN_and_Viet_Cong) attacks as well as targeting the pacification and Vietnamization strategy.[^cite_note-Currey-269] Following Tet, the PAVN had transformed from a [light-infantry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_infantry), limited mobility force into [high-mobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maneuver_warfare) and mechanized [combined arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_arms).[^cite_note-Currey-269]<sup>: 189 </sup>

### US domestic controversies

The [anti-war movement](39766716.md) was gaining strength in the US. Nixon appealed to the "[silent majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silent_majority)" who he said supported the war. But revelations of the 1968 [My Lai massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/My_Lai_massacre),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018518–521-271] in which a US Army unit raped and killed more than 500 Vietnamese [civilians](145804.md),[^cite_note-272] and the 1969 "[Green Beret Affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Beret_Affair)", where [Special Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Army_Special_Forces) soldiers were arrested for the murder[^cite_note-273] of a suspected double agent,[^cite_note-274] provoked outrage.

In 1971, the *[Pentagon Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentagon_Papers)* were leaked to *[The New York Times](30680.md)*. The secret history of US involvement, commissioned by the Department of Defense, detailed public deceptions by the government. The [Supreme Court](31737.md) ruled publication legal.[^cite_note-275]

### Collapsing US morale

Further information: [G.I. movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G.I._movement)

Following Tet and decreasing public support, US forces began a period of morale collapse, and disobedience.[^cite_note-Stewart-276]<sup>: 349–350 </sup>[^cite_note-Daddis-277]<sup>: 166–175 </sup> At home, desertion rates quadrupled from 1966 levels.[^cite_note-Heinl-278] Among the enlisted, only 2.5% chose infantry between 1969 and 1970.[^cite_note-Heinl-278] [ROTC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_Officers'_Training_Corps) enrollment decreased from 191,749 in 1966 to 72,459 by 1971,[^cite_note-279] and a low of 33,220 in 1974,[^cite_note-280] depriving the US of much-needed military leadership.
