Refusal to engage in patrols or carry out orders emerged, with a case of an entire company refusing orders.[^cite_note-281] Unit cohesion began to dissipate and focused on minimizing contact with the PAVN/VC.[^cite_note-Daddis-277] A practice known as "sand-bagging" started, where units ordered to patrol would go into the country-side, find a site out of view from superiors and radio in false coordinates and reports.[^cite_note-Ward-226]<sup>: 407–411 </sup> Drug usage increased, 30% regularly used marijuana,[^cite_note-Ward-226]<sup>: 407 </sup> while a House subcommittee found 10% used heroin.[^cite_note-Heinl-278][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018526-282] From 1969, search-and-destroy operations became referred to as "search and avoid", falsifying battle reports while avoiding guerrillas.[^cite_note-283] 900 [fragging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fragging) (killing a fellow officer, usually superior) and suspected fragging incidents were investigated, most occurring between 1969 and 1971.[^cite_note-Stanton-284]<sup>: 331 </sup>[^cite_note-Ward-226]<sup>: 407 </sup> In 1969, field-performance was characterized by low morale and poor leadership.[^cite_note-Stanton-284]<sup>: 331 </sup> The decline in morale was demonstrated by the [Battle of FSB Mary Ann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_FSB_Mary_Ann) in 1971, in which a sapper attack inflicted serious losses on US defenders.[^cite_note-Stanton-284]<sup>: 357 </sup> Westmoreland, no longer in command but tasked with investigation of the failure, cited dereliction of duty, lax defensive postures and lack of officers in charge.[^cite_note-Stanton-284]<sup>: 357 </sup>

On the collapse of morale, Shelby Stanton wrote:

> In the last years of the Army's retreat, its remaining forces were relegated to static security. The American Army's decline was readily apparent. ... Racial incidents, drug abuse, combat disobedience, and crime reflected growing idleness, resentment ... the fatal handicaps of faulty campaign strategy, incomplete wartime preparation, and the tardy, superficial attempts at Vietnamization. An entire American army was sacrificed on the battlefield of Vietnam.[^cite_note-Stanton-284]<sup>: 366–368 </sup>

### Vietnamization

See also: [Vietnamization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamization)



ARVN and US Special Forces, 1968

Beginning in 1969, American troops were withdrawn from border areas where most of the fighting took place and redeployed along the coast and interior. US casualties in 1970 were less than half of 1969, after being relegated to less active combat.[^cite_note-upi1970-285] While US forces were redeployed, the ARVN took over combat operations, with casualties double US ones in 1969, and triple in 1970.[^cite_note-Wiest-286] Post-Tet, membership in the [South Vietnamese Regional Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Vietnamese_Regional_Forces) and [Popular Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Vietnamese_Popular_Force) militias grew, and they could now provide village security, which the Americans had not accomplished.[^cite_note-Wiest-286]

In 1970, Nixon announced the withdrawal of an additional 150,000 American troops, reducing US numbers to 265,500.[^cite_note-upi1970-285] By 1970, VC forces were no longer southern-majority, nearly 70% were northerners.[^cite_note-287] Between 1969 and 1971 the VC and some PAVN units had reverted to [small unit tactics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_unit_tactics), instead of nationwide offensives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENguyen2012-288] In 1971, Australia and New Zealand withdrew their soldiers and US troops were reduced to 196,700, with a deadline to remove another 45,000 troops by February 1972. The US reduced support troops, and in March 1971 the [5th Special Forces Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5th_Special_Forces_Group_\(United_States\)), the first American unit deployed, withdrew.[^cite_note-StantonVOB-289]<sup>: 240 </sup>[^cite_note-292]

### Cambodia

Main articles: [Operation Menu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Menu), [Operation Freedom Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Freedom_Deal), and [Cambodian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_Civil_War)



An alleged Viet Cong captured during an attack on an American outpost near the Cambodian border is interrogated.

Prince [Norodom Sihanouk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norodom_Sihanouk) had proclaimed Cambodia neutral since 1955,[^cite_note-293] but permitted the PAVN/VC to use the port of [Sihanoukville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sihanoukville_Autonomous_Port) and [Sihanouk Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sihanouk_Trail). In March 1969 Nixon launched a secret bombing campaign, called [Operation Menu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Menu), against communist sanctuaries along the Cambodia/Vietnam border. Only five congressional officials were informed.[^cite_note-294]

In March 1970, [Sihanouk was deposed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_coup_of_1970) by his [pro-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-American) prime minister [Lon Nol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lon_Nol), who demanded North Vietnamese troops leave Cambodia or face military action.[^cite_note-295] Nol began rounding up Vietnamese civilians in Cambodia and massacring them, provoking reactions from the North and South Vietnamese governments.[^cite_note-296] In April–May 1970, North Vietnam invaded Cambodia at the request of the [Khmer Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_Rouge), following negotiations with deputy leader [Nuon Chea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuon_Chea). Nguyen Co Thach recalls: "Nuon Chea has asked for help and we have liberated five provinces of Cambodia in ten days."[^cite_note-297] US and ARVN forces launched the [Cambodian campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_campaign) in May to attack PAVN/VC bases. A counter-offensive in 1971, as part of [Operation Chenla II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Chenla_II) by the PAVN, recaptured most border areas and decimated Nol's forces.

The US incursion into Cambodia sparked [US protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protests_against_the_Vietnam_War) as Nixon had promised to deescalate involvement. [Students were killed by National Guardsmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kent_State_shootings) in May 1970 at a [Kent State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kent_State_University) protest, which provoked further outrage. The administration's reaction was seen as callous, reinvigorating the anti-war movement.[^cite_note-Daddis-277]<sup>: 128–129 </sup> The US continued to bomb Cambodia as part of [Operation Freedom Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Freedom_Deal).

### Laos

Main articles: [Operation Commando Hunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Commando_Hunt), [Laotian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laotian_Civil_War), and [Operation Lam Son 719](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Lam_Son_719)

Building on the success of ARVN units in Cambodia, and further testing the Vietnamization program, the ARVN was tasked with [Operation Lam Son 719](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Lam_Son_719) in February 1971, the first major ground operation to attack and interdict the Ho Chi Minh Trail. This was the first time the PAVN would field-test its combined arms forces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENguyen2012-288] The first few days were a success, but momentum slowed after fierce resistance. Thiệu halted the general advance, enabling PAVN armored divisions to surround them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWillbanks201489-298]

Thieu ordered [air assault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_assault) troops to capture the Tchepone crossroad and withdraw, despite facing forces outnumbering them by four times. During the withdrawal, a PAVN counterattack forced the retreating ARVN into a panicked rout. Half of the ARVN troops were either captured or killed, and half the ARVN/US helicopters were downed. The operation was a disaster and demonstrated the operational deficiencies within the ARVN.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997644–645-299] Nixon and Thieu had sought a showcase victory simply by capturing Tchepone, and it was spun off as an "operational success".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWillbanks2014118-300][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018576–582-301]

### Easter Offensive and Paris Peace Accords, 1972



PAVN artillery bombards ARVN positions near Kon Tum. 1972.

In 1972, North Vietnam launched a conventional invasion of South Vietnam in the [Easter Offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Offensive) with 300,000 troops and hundreds of tanks. The PAVN overran the northern provinces and attacked towards Saigon from Cambodia, threatening to cut the country in half. The fighting was characterized by vicious fighting between PAVN and ARVN formations, especially in [An Loc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_An_L%E1%BB%99c) and [Kontum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kontum).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018610–630-302]

Although the US was withdrawing their troops from the country, it provided critical air support for ARVN's defense of the South Vietnam, beginning [Operation Linebacker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Linebacker). The offensive was halted with heavy casualties on both sides, estimated at over 200,000 soldiers and civilians for both sides.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018606–637-303] The US Navy initiated [Operation Pocket Money](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Pocket_Money) in May, an aerial mining campaign in [Haiphong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haiphong) Harbor that prevented North Vietnam's allies from resupplying it with weapons.[^cite_note-304]



Soviet advisers inspecting the debris of a B-52 downed in the vicinity of Hanoi

The war was central to the [1972 US presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_United_States_presidential_election) as Nixon's opponent, [George McGovern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_McGovern), campaigned on immediate withdrawal. Nixon's Security Advisor, [Henry Kissinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Kissinger), had continued secret negotiations with North Vietnam's [Lê Đức Thọ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%AA_%C4%90%E1%BB%A9c_Th%E1%BB%8D) and in October 1972 reached an agreement. Thiệu demanded changes to the peace accord upon its discovery, and when North Vietnam went public with the details, the Nixon administration claimed they were attempting to embarrass the president. The negotiations became deadlocked when Hanoi demanded changes. To show his support for South Vietnam and force Hanoi back to the negotiating table, Nixon ordered [Operation Linebacker II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Linebacker_II), a bombing of Hanoi and Haiphong in December.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018649–663-305] Nixon pressured Thiệu to accept the agreement or face military action.[^cite_note-306]

On 15 January 1973, all US combat activities were suspended. Lê Đức Thọ and Henry Kissinger, along with the PRG Foreign Minister [Nguyễn Thị Bình](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nguy%E1%BB%85n_Th%E1%BB%8B_B%C3%ACnh) and a reluctant Thiệu, signed the [Paris Peace Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Peace_Accords) on 27 January.[^cite_note-Ward-226]<sup>: 508–513 </sup> This ended direct US involvement in the war, created a ceasefire between North Vietnam/PRG and South Vietnam, guaranteed the territorial integrity of Vietnam under the Geneva Conference, called for elections or a political settlement between the PRG and South Vietnam, allowed 200,000 communist troops to remain in the south, and agreed to a POW exchange. There was a 60-day period for the withdrawal of US forces. "This article proved ... to be the only one ... which was fully carried out."[^cite_note-307] All US forces personnel were withdrawn by March.[^cite_note-Herring-110]<sup>: 260 </sup>

## US exit and final campaigns, 1973–1975



American POWs recently released from North Vietnamese prison camps, 1973

In the lead-up to the ceasefire on 28 January, both sides attempted to maximize land and population under their control in a campaign known as the [War of the Flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Flags). Fighting continued after the ceasefire, without US participation, and throughout the year.[^cite_note-Ward-226]<sup>: 508–513 </sup>

On 15 March 1973, Nixon implied the US would intervene militarily if the North launched a full offensive, and Defense Secretary [Schlesinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_R._Schlesinger) re-affirmed this during his confirmation hearings. Public and congressional reaction was unfavorable, prompting the Senate to pass the [Case–Church Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case%E2%80%93Church_Amendment) to prohibit intervention.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997670–672-308]

Northern leaders expected the ceasefire terms would favor their side, but Saigon, bolstered by a surge of US aid just before the ceasefire, began to roll them back. The North responded with a new strategy developed in March 1973, according to [Trần Văn Trà](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tr%E1%BA%A7n_V%C4%83n_Tr%C3%A0).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997672–674-309] With US bombings suspended, work on the Ho Chi Minh Trail and other logistical structures could proceed. Logistics would be upgraded until the North was in a position to launch a massive invasion of the South, projected for the 1975–76 dry season. Trà calculated this would be Hanoi's last opportunity to strike, before Saigon's army could be fully trained.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997672–674-309]



Memorial commemorating the 1974 [Buon Me Thuot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buon_Me_Thuot) campaign, depicting a [Montagnard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Degar) of the [Central Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Highlands,_Vietnam), a NVA soldier and a [T-54 tank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-54/T-55)

In South Vietnam, the US departure, and recession after the [1973 oil crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_oil_crisis), hurt an economy dependent on US support and troop presence. After clashes that left 55 ARVN soldiers dead, Thiệu announced in January 1974, that the war had restarted and the Peace Accords were no longer in effect. There were over 25,000 South Vietnamese casualties during the ceasefire period.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018683-310]<sup>: 683 </sup> [Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford) took over as US president in August 1974, and Congress cut financial aid to South Vietnam from \$1 billion a year to \$700 million. Congress voted funding restrictions to be phased in through 1975 and total cutoff in 1976.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018686-311]

The success of the 1973–74 dry season offensive inspired Trà to return to Hanoi in October 1974 and plead for a larger offensive the next dry season. This time, Trà could travel on a drivable highway with fueling stops, a vast change from when the Ho Chi Minh Trail was a dangerous mountain trek.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997676-312] Giáp, the North Vietnamese defense minister, was reluctant to approve Trà's plan since a large offensive might provoke US reaction and interfere with the big push planned for 1976. Trà appealed to Giáp's superior, Lê Duẩn, who approved it. Trà's plan called for a limited offensive from Cambodia into [Phước Long province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ph%C6%B0%E1%BB%9Bc_Long_province). The strike was designed to solve logistical problems, gauge the reaction of South Vietnamese forces, and determine whether the US would return.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018685–690-313] On 13 December 1974, PAVN forces [attacked Phước Long](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Phuoc_Long). Phuoc Binh fell on 6 January 1975. Ford desperately asked Congress for funds to assist and re-supply the South before it was overrun. Congress refused. The fall of Phuoc Binh and lack of American response left the South Vietnamese elite demoralized.

The speed of this success led the Politburo to reassess. It decided operations in the Central Highlands would be turned over to General Văn Tiến Dũng and [Pleiku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pleiku) should be seized, if possible. Dũng said to Lê Duẩn: "Never have we had military and political conditions so perfect or a strategic advantage as great as we have now."[^cite_note-314] At the start of 1975, the South Vietnamese had three times as much artillery and twice as many tanks and armored vehicles as the PAVN. However, heightened oil prices meant many assets could not be used. Moreover, the rushed nature of Vietnamization, intended to cover US retreat, resulted in lack of spare parts, ground-crew, and maintenance personnel, which rendered most of it inoperable.[^cite_note-Stewart-276]<sup>: 362–366 </sup>

### Campaign 275

See also: [1975 spring offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_spring_offensive), [Battle of Ban Me Thuot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ban_Me_Thuot), and [Hue–Da Nang Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hue%E2%80%93Da_Nang_Campaign)



The capture of Hue, March 1975

On 10 March 1975, Dũng launched Campaign 275, a limited offensive into the Central Highlands, supported by tanks and heavy artillery. The target was [Ban Ma Thuột](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ban_Me_Thuot); if the town could be taken, the provincial capital Pleiku and the road to the coast, would be exposed for a campaign in 1976. The ARVN proved incapable of resisting, its forces collapsed. Again, Hanoi was surprised by the speed of its success. Dung urged the Politburo to allow him to seize Pleiku immediately and turn his attention to [Kon Tum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kon_Tum). He argued that with two months of good weather until the monsoon, it would be irresponsible not to take advantage.[^cite_note-Tucker-8]

Thiệu ordered the abandonment of the Central Highlands and less defensible positions in a rushed policy described as "light at the top, heavy at the bottom". While most ARVN forces attempted to flee, isolated units fought desperately. ARVN general Phu abandoned Pleiku and Kon Tum and retreated toward the coast, in the "convoy of tears".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018693–694-315] On 20 March, Thiệu reversed himself and ordered Huế, Vietnam's third-largest city, held at all costs, and then changed policy several times. As the PAVN attacked, panic set in, and ARVN resistance withered. On 22 March, the PAVN [attacked Huế](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hue%E2%80%93Da_Nang_Campaign). By 30 March 100,000 leaderless ARVN troops surrendered as the PAVN marched through Da Nang. With the fall of the city, the defense of the Central Highlands and Northern provinces ended.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018699–700-316]

### Final North Vietnamese offensive

Further information: [Ho Chi Minh Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho_Chi_Minh_Campaign)

See also: [Battle of Xuân Lộc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Xu%C3%A2n_L%E1%BB%99c)



South Vietnamese soldiers during the [battle of Xuân Lộc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Xu%C3%A2n_L%E1%BB%99c), April 1975

With the north half of the country under their control, the Politburo ordered Dũng to launch the final offensive. The operational plan for the [Ho Chi Minh Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho_Chi_Minh_Campaign) called for Saigon's capture before 1 May. Hanoi wished to avoid the monsoon and prevent redeployment of ARVN forces defending the capital. PAVN forces, their morale boosted by recent victories, rolled on, taking [Nha Trang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nha_Trang), [Cam Ranh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cam_Ranh) and [Da Lat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da_Lat).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018702–704-317]

On 7 April, three PAVN divisions attacked [Xuân Lộc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Xu%C3%A2n_L%E1%BB%99c), 40 miles (64 km) northeast of Saigon. Fighting raged as the ARVN defenders made a [last stand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_stand) to block PAVN advance. On 21 April however, the exhausted garrison was ordered to withdraw towards Saigon.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018704–707-318] An embittered and tearful Thiệu resigned, declaring that the US had betrayed South Vietnam. He suggested Kissinger had tricked him into signing the Paris Accords, promising military aid that failed to materialize. Having transferred power to [Trần Văn Hương](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tr%E1%BA%A7n_V%C4%83n_H%C6%B0%C6%A1ng) on 21 April, he left for [Taiwan](25734.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018714-319] After having appealed unsuccessfully to Congress for \$722 million in emergency aid for South Vietnam, Ford gave a televised speech on 23 April, declaring an end to the war and US aid.[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321]

By the end of April, the ARVN had collapsed except in the [Mekong Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mekong_Delta). Refugees streamed southward, ahead of the PAVN onslaught. By 27 April, 100,000 PAVN troops encircled Saigon. The city was defended by about 30,000 ARVN troops. To hasten a collapse and foment panic, the PAVN shelled [Tan Son Nhut Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tan_Son_Nhut_Airport) and forced its closure. Large numbers of civilians had no way out.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018716-322]

### Fall of Saigon

Main articles: [Fall of Saigon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Saigon) and [Operation Frequent Wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Frequent_Wind)



Victorious PAVN troops at the Presidential Palace, Saigon

[Martial law](50477.md) was declared as American helicopters began evacuating South Vietnamese, US and foreign nationals from Tan Son Nhut and the embassy compound. [Operation Frequent Wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Frequent_Wind) had been delayed until the last possible moment, because of Ambassador [Graham Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graham_Martin)'s belief Saigon could be held and a political settlement reached. Frequent Wind was the largest helicopter evacuation in history. In the morning of 30 April, the last US Marines evacuated the embassy by helicopter, as civilians swamped the perimeter and poured into the grounds.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018718–720-323]

PAVN troops entered Saigon and overcame all resistance, capturing key buildings and installations.[^cite_note-mtholyoke.edu-324] Tanks from the [2nd Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_Corps_\(Vietnam\)) crashed through the gates of the [Independence Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Palace) and the VC flag was raised above it.[^cite_note-325] President Dương Văn Minh, who had succeeded Huong two days earlier, surrendered to Lieutenant colonel Bùi Văn Tùng, political commissar of the 203rd Tank Brigade.[^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327][^cite_note-Terzani-328]<sup>: 95–96 </sup> Minh was escorted to [Radio Saigon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Saigon) to announce the surrender.[^cite_note-Bui_Tin-329]<sup>: 85 </sup> The statement was on air at 2\:30 pm.[^cite_note-Terzani-328]

## Opposition to US involvement

Main articles: [Opposition to United States involvement in the Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_to_United_States_involvement_in_the_Vietnam_War) and [Protests of 1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protests_of_1968)

See also: [Russell Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russell_Tribunal), [Fulbright hearings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fulbright_hearings), and [Chicago Seven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Seven)



The [1967 March on the Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_March_on_the_Pentagon), an antiwar demonstration organized by the [National Mobilization Committee to End the War in Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Mobilization_Committee_to_End_the_War_in_Vietnam)

During the war, opposition to US involvement increased among segments of the American public, although many did not identify with the antiwar movement.[^cite_note-Hall2024-330]<sup>: 402 </sup> In January 1967, 32% of Americans thought the US had made a mistake in sending troops.[^cite_note-331] Opinion steadily turned following 1967 and by 1970, 60% believed the US had made a mistake.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-Hagopian-333] Many young people opposed US involvement because they were being [drafted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_the_United_States), others because the antiwar movement grew popular among the [counterculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterculture_of_the_1960s). Opposition to the war tended to unite groups opposed to US anticommunism and [imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_imperialism),[^cite_note-334] and for those involved with the [New Left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Left).

High-profile opposition increasingly turned to mass protests to shift opinion. Riots broke out at the [1968 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Democratic_National_Convention).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018514-335] After reports of American military abuses brought attention and support to the antiwar movement, some veterans joined [Vietnam Veterans Against the War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_Veterans_Against_the_War). In October 1969, the [Vietnam Moratorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moratorium_to_End_the_War_in_Vietnam) attracted millions.[^cite_note-336] The fatal shooting of students at Kent State University in 1970 led to nationwide university protests.[^cite_note-337] Antiwar protests declined after the Paris Peace Accords, the [end of the draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_the_United_States#End_of_conscription), and US troop withdrawal in 1973.

Polls indicated that the antiwar movement remained broadly unpopular even as support for the war declined. Historians remain divided over the extent of the movement's influence on the outcome of the conflict. Some have argued that growing public opposition to the war was driven primarily by its mounting human and economic costs and the perceived military stalemate, rather than by antiwar activism.[^cite_note-Hall2024-330]<sup>: 401–402 </sup>

## Involvement of other countries

Main article: [International participation in the Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_participation_in_the_Vietnam_War)

### Pro-Hanoi

#### China

See also: [China in the Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_in_the_Vietnam_War)

Strategic security and ideology were the two main determinants of China's approach to the Indochina conflict. As Mao's emphasis shifted between these two factors over time, China's policy changed.[^cite_note-YKS-338]<sup>: 88 </sup> During the First Indochina War, China became the Viet Minh's principal supporter, providing substantially more aid than the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESzalontai2005412-339] During the [1954 Geneva Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Geneva_Conference), concerned about the prospect of another direct confrontation with Washington, as had occurred in Korea, Beijing favored a ceasefire and, like Moscow, pressured the Viet Minh to accept the temporary partition of Vietnam.[^cite_note-YKS-338]<sup>: 60 </sup>

Between 1958 and 1969, however, China embraced an [anti-revisionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-revisionism) stance and bolstered armed struggle in South Vietnam. From 1969 onward, national security and strategic considerations led China to adopt a more flexible foreign policy, seeking an alliance with the US against the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-YKS-338]<sup>: 90 </sup>

China said its military and economic aid to North Vietnam totaled \$160 billion (adjusted for 2022 prices);[^cite_note-Womack-7] included were 5 million tons of food (equivalent to a year's production), accounting for 10–15% of the North's food supply by the 1970s.[^cite_note-Womack-7] In summer 1962, [Mao Zedong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong) agreed to supply Hanoi with 90,000 rifles and guns free of charge, and starting in 1965, China began sending [anti-aircraft](146640.md) units and engineering battalions, to repair damage caused by American bombing. They helped man anti-aircraft batteries, rebuild roads and railroads, transport supplies, and perform other engineering works. This freed PAVN units for combat. China sent 320,000 troops and annual arms shipments worth \$180 million.[^cite_note-Qiang-340]<sup>: 135 </sup> China claims to have caused 38% of American air losses.[^cite_note-Womack-7] China began financing the Khmer Rouge as a counterweight to North Vietnam. China "armed and trained" the Khmer Rouge during the civil war, and continued to aid them afterward.[^cite_note-341]

#### Soviet Union

For further reading, see [Bibliography of the post-Stalinist Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibliography_of_the_post-Stalinist_Soviet_Union)



Soviet [air defense](146640.md) instructors and North Vietnamese crewmen in the spring of 1965 at an air defense training center in Vietnam

The Soviet Union supplied North Vietnam with medical supplies, arms, tanks, planes, helicopters, artillery, anti-aircraft missiles and other military equipment. Soviet crews fired Soviet-made [surface-to-air missiles](182664.md) at US aircraft in 1965.[^cite_note-342] Soviet pilots sometimes flew the planes their government supplied.[^cite_note-Lind1999-219]<sup>: 89 </sup>[^cite_note-343] Following the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) in 1991, Russia acknowledged that the USSR had stationed up to 3,000 troops in Vietnam.[^cite_note-344] 16 Soviet military personnel were killed during the war.[^cite_note-LMN-10][^cite_note-Dunnigan_&_Nofi_284-345]

Between 1953 and 1991, the hardware donated by the Soviet Union included: 2,000 tanks; 1,700 [APCs](19278686.md); 7,000 artillery guns; over 5,000 anti-aircraft guns; 158 surface-to-air missile launchers; and 120 helicopters. The Soviets sent North Vietnam annual arms shipments worth \$450 million.[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018364–371-347] From 1965 to 1974, fighting in Vietnam was observed by 11,000 military personnel of the [Soviet Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Armed_Forces).[^cite_note-348] The [KGB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGB) helped develop the [signals intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Signals_intelligence) capabilities of the North Vietnamese.[^cite_note-MP-349]

### Pro-Saigon

See also: [Southeast Asia Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia_Treaty_Organization) and [Many Flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Many_Flags)

As South Vietnam was formally part of a military alliance with the US, Australia, New Zealand, France, the UK, Pakistan, Thailand and the Philippines, the alliance was invoked during the war. The UK, France and Pakistan declined to participate, and South Korea, Taiwan, and Spain were non-treaty participants.

## United Front for the Liberation of Oppressed Races

Main articles: [United Front for the Liberation of Oppressed Races](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Front_for_the_Liberation_of_Oppressed_Races) and [FULRO insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FULRO_insurgency)

The ethnic minorities of South Vietnam, like the [Montagnards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montagnard_\(Vietnam\)) in the Central Highlands, the Hindu and Muslim [Cham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chams), and the Buddhist [Khmer Krom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_Krom), were actively recruited in the war. There was a strategy of recruitment and favorable treatment of Montagnard tribes by the VC, as they were pivotal for control of infiltration routes.[^cite_note-350] Some groups split off and formed the *United Front for the Liberation of Oppressed Races* (FULRO) to fight for autonomy or independence. FULRO fought against the South Vietnamese and VC, later fighting against the unified [Socialist Republic of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Republic_of_Vietnam), after the fall of South Vietnam.

## War crimes

Main articles: [List of war crimes § 1955–1975: Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_war_crimes#1955%E2%80%931975:_Vietnam_War), and [List of massacres in Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_massacres_in_Vietnam)

Both sides committed numerous [war crimes](48626.md) including [rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rape_during_the_Vietnam_War), massacring civilians, bombings of civilian targets, [terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NLF_and_PAVN_strategy,_organization_and_structure#Use_of_terror), torture, and summarily executing [prisoners of war](25008.md). Other crimes included theft, arson, and the destruction of property not warranted by [military necessity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_necessity).[^cite_note-351]

### &#x20;South Vietnamese, American and South Korean

See also: [United States war crimes § Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_war_crimes#Vietnam_War), [Winter Soldier Investigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_Soldier_Investigation), [Vietnam War Crimes Working Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War_Crimes_Working_Group), and [Tiger Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiger_Force)



Victims of the [My Lai massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/My_Lai_massacre)

In 1966, the [Russell Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russell_Tribunal) was organized by public figures opposed to the war led by [Bertrand Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bertrand_Russell), to apply the precepts of [international law](8195726.md). The tribunal found the US and allies guilty of [acts of aggression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_aggression), use of weapons forbidden by the laws of war, bombardment of targets of a purely civilian character, mistreatment of prisoners, and [genocide](12441.md). Though the tribunal's lack of juridical authority meant findings were ignored by the US and other governments, the hearings contributed to growing evidence which established the factual basis for a counter-narrative to US justifications for the war and inspired hearings, tribunals and legal investigations.[^cite_note-352]

In 1968, the [Vietnam War Crimes Working Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War_Crimes_Working_Group) (VWCWG) was established by the Pentagon [task force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Task_force) set up in the wake of the My Lai massacre, to ascertain the veracity of claims of [US war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_war_crimes). Of the crimes reported to military authorities, sworn statements by witnesses and status reports indicated 320 incidents had a factual basis.[^cite_note-TurseNelson-353] The substantiated cases included seven massacres between 1967 and 1971 in which at least 137 civilians were killed; 78 further attacks targeting non-combatants resulting in at least 57 deaths and 15 sexually assaulted; and 141 cases of US soldiers torturing civilian detainees, or prisoners of war, with fists, sticks, bats, water or electric shock. Journalists have documented uninvestigated war crimes, involving every active army division,[^cite_note-TurseNelson-353] including atrocities committed by [Tiger Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiger_Force).[^cite_note-354] [R. J. Rummel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R._J._Rummel) estimated that American forces committed around 5,500 [democidal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democide) killings between 1960 and 1972.[^cite_note-Rummel-29]

US forces established [free-fire zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free-fire_zone) to prevent VC fighters from sheltering in South Vietnamese villages.[^cite_note-355] Such practice, which involved the assumption that anyone appearing in the designated zones was an enemy that could be freely targeted, was regarded as "a severe violation of the laws of war".[^cite_note-356] [Nick Turse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Turse) argues that a drive toward higher [body counts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body_count), widespread use of free-fire zones, rules of engagement where civilians who ran from soldiers or helicopters could be viewed as VC and disdain for civilians, led to massive civilian casualties and war crimes.[^cite_note-Turse-357]<sup>: 251 </sup> An example cited by Turse is [Operation Speedy Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Speedy_Express), described by [John Paul Vann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Paul_Vann) as "many Mỹ Lais".[^cite_note-Turse-357]<sup>: 251 </sup> A report by *Newsweek* suggested at least 5,000 civilians may have been killed during the operation, and an official US military body count of 10,889 enemy combatants killed.[^cite_note-358]



"[The Terror of War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Terror_of_War)" by [Nick Ut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Ut), which won the 1973 [Pulitzer Prize for Spot News Photography](24230.md), showing [a nine-year-old girl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phan_Thi_Kim_Phuc) running down a road after being severely burned by napalm

Rummel estimated 39,000 were killed by South Vietnam during the Diem-era in democide; for 1964–1975, Rummel estimated 50,000 people were killed in democide. Thus, the total for 1954–1975 is about 80,000 deaths caused by South Vietnam.[^cite_note-Rummel-29] [Benjamin Valentino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Valentino) estimates 110,000–310,000 deaths as a "possible case" of "counter-guerrilla mass killings" by US and South Vietnamese forces.[^cite_note-Valentino-359] The [Phoenix Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Program), coordinated by the CIA, was aimed at destroying the political infrastructure of the VC. The program killed 26,000–41,000 people, an unknown number were innocent civilians.[^cite_note-Ward-226]<sup>: 341–343 </sup>[^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361][^cite_note-362]

Torture and ill-treatment were frequently applied by the South Vietnamese to POWs, as well as civilian prisoners.[^cite_note-Greiner-363]<sup>: 77 </sup> During their visit to the [Con Son Prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Con_Dao_Prison) in 1970, congressmen [Augustus Hawkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustus_Hawkins) and [William R. Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Anderson_\(naval_officer\)) witnessed detainees either confined in minute "tiger cages" or chained to their cells, and provided with poor-quality food. American doctors found inmates suffering symptoms resulting from torture.[^cite_note-Greiner-363]<sup>: 77 </sup> During their visits to US detention facilities, the [International Red Cross](15487.md) recorded many cases of torture and inhumane treatment.[^cite_note-Greiner-363]<sup>: 78 </sup> Torture was conducted by the South Vietnamese in collusion with the CIA.[^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365] Unlike massacres such as My Lai, media reports of torture of POWs by South Vietnamese and US forces did not generate significant public outcry in the US.[^cite_note-366]

South Korean forces were accused of war crimes. One event was the 1968 [Phong Nhị and Phong Nhất massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phong_Nh%E1%BB%8B_and_Phong_Nh%E1%BA%A5t_massacre) where the [2nd Marine Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_Marine_Division_\(South_Korea\)) reportedly killed about 70 civilians.[^cite_note-367] South Korean forces are accused of perpetrating the [Bình Hòa massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%ACnh_H%C3%B2a_massacre), [Binh Tai massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binh_Tai_massacre) and [Hà My massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%A0_My_massacre).

### North Vietnamese and Viet Cong

Main article: [Viet Cong and People's Army of Vietnam use of terror in the Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viet_Cong_and_People's_Army_of_Vietnam_use_of_terror_in_the_Vietnam_War)

See also: [Cambodian Civil War § War crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_Civil_War#War_crimes)



Victims killed in a Viet Cong mine explosion in Phú Yên, 1965

"The overall volume and lethality of Viet Cong terrorism rivals or exceeds all but a handful of terrorist campaigns waged over the last third of the twentieth century", based on the definition of terrorists as a non-state actor, and examining targeted killings and civilian deaths which are estimated at over 18,000 from 1966 to 1969.[^cite_note-368] The Pentagon estimates the VC/PAVN conducted 36,000 murders and 58,000 kidnappings from 1967 to 1972, c. 1973.[^cite_note-369] Benjamin Valentino attributes 45,000–80,000 "terrorist mass killings" to the VC.[^cite_note-Valentino-359] Statistics for 1968–72 suggest "about 80 percent of the terrorist victims were ordinary civilians and only about 20 percent were government officials, policemen, members of the self-defence forces or pacification cadres".[^cite_note-Lewy-19]<sup>: 273 </sup>



Mourning the remains of victims discovered at Đá Mài Creek in 1969, one year after the [Huế massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre_at_Hu%E1%BA%BF)

The VC also used terror to spread and maintain their control on the countryside. Max Hastings writes that two-thirds of the VC's general support stemmed from fear. For example, in 1960, VC cadres summarily executed alleged "traitors" with machetes, live burial, and beheadings. In one case, they disemboweled a village mayor and his wife, while also beheading his children.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018124-370]

VC tactics included frequent mortaring of civilians in refugee camps, and placing of mines on highways frequented by villagers taking goods to urban markets. Some mines were set only to go off after heavy vehicle passage, causing slaughter aboard packed buses.[^cite_note-Lewy-19]<sup>: 270–279 </sup>

Notable VC atrocities include the massacre of over 3,000 unarmed civilians at Huế[^cite_note-371] and killing 252 Montagnard civilians during the [Đắk Sơn massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%90%E1%BA%AFk_S%C6%A1n_massacre).[^cite_note-372] 155,000 refugees fleeing the North Vietnamese Spring Offensive were reported to have been killed, or abducted, on the road to [Tuy Hòa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuy_H%C3%B2a) in 1975.[^cite_note-373] PAVN/VC troops killed 164,000 civilians in democide between 1954 and 1975 in South Vietnam.[^cite_note-Rummel-29] North Vietnam was known for its abusive treatment of American POWs, most notably in [Hỏa Lò Prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%E1%BB%8Fa_L%C3%B2_Prison) (the 'Hanoi Hilton'), where torture was employed to [extract confessions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_confession).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow1997655-374]

## Women

Main article: [Women in the Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_the_Vietnam_War)



A nurse treats a Vietnamese child, 1967

Women were active in a variety of roles, making significant impacts and the war having significant impacts on them.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377] Several million Vietnamese women served in the military and militias, particularly in the VC, with the slogan "when war comes, even the women must fight" widely used.[^cite_note-378] These women made vital contributions on the Ho Chi Minh Trail, espionage, medical care, logistical and administrative work, and direct combat.[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380] Women took on more roles in the economy and Vietnam saw an increase in women's rights.[^cite_note-Werner-381] Women emerged as leaders of anti-war campaigns and made significant contributions to [war journalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_journalism).[^cite_note-382]

Women still faced discrimination, and were targets of [sexual violence](17572536.md) and [war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_war_crimes#1955%E2%80%931975:_Vietnam_War).[^cite_note-383] Post-war, Vietnamese women veterans faced difficulty reintegrating into society and having their contributions recognised, as well as advances in rights failing to be sustained.[^cite_note-Lamb_2003-384][^cite_note-385] Portrayals of the war have been criticised for their depictions of women, for overlooking the role women played and reducing Vietnamese women to racist stereotypes.[^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387] Women are at the forefront of campaigns to deal with the war's aftermath, such as the effect of [Agent Orange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agent_Orange) use and the [Lai Đại Hàn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lai_%C4%90%E1%BA%A1i_H%C3%A0n).[^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390]

## Black servicemen

Main article: [Military history of African Americans in the Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_African_Americans_in_the_Vietnam_War)



A wounded Black American soldier being evacuated during the My Lai Massacre

The experience of Black American military personnel has received significant attention.[^cite_note-391] [Wallace Terry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallace_Terry)'s work includes observations about the impacts on black servicemen. He notes the higher proportion of combat casualties among African-American servicemen, the shift toward and different attitudes of black military volunteers and conscripts, the discrimination encountered "on the battlefield, in decorations, promotion and duty assignments", as well as having to endure "the racial insults, cross-burnings and Confederate flags of their white comrades"—and the experiences faced by black soldiers stateside, during the war and afterwards.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETerry1984Epigraph,_pp._xv–xvii-392]

Civil rights leaders protested the disproportionate casualties and overrepresentation in hazardous duty experienced by black servicemen, prompting reforms implemented from 1967. As a result, by the war's completion, black casualties had declined to 13% of combat deaths, about equal to the share of draft-eligible black men, though slightly higher than the 10% who served.[^cite_note-393]

## Weapons

Main article: [List of weapons of the Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_weapons_of_the_Vietnam_War)



Guerrillas assemble shells and rockets delivered along the Ho Chi Minh Trail.

Nearly all US-allied forces were armed with US weapons including the [M1 Garand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M1_Garand), [M1 carbine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M1_carbine), [M14 rifle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M14_rifle), and [M16 rifle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M16_rifle). The Australian and New Zealand forces employed the 7.62 mm [L1A1 Self-Loading Rifle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L1A1_Self-Loading_Rifle),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETucker2011975-394] with occasional use of the M16 rifle.

The PAVN/VC, although having inherited US, French, and Japanese weapons from World War II and the [First Indochina War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Indochina_War), were largely armed and supplied by China, the Soviet Union, and its [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) allies. Some weapons—notably anti-personnel explosives, the [K-50M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K-50M), and "home-made" versions of the [RPG-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RPG-2)—were manufactured in North Vietnam. By 1969 the US Army had identified 40 rifle/carbine types, 22 machine gun types, 17 types of mortar, 20 recoilless rifle or rocket launcher types, nine types of antitank weapons, and 14 anti-aircraft artillery weapons used by ground troops on all sides. Also in use, mostly by anti-communist forces, were 24 types of armored vehicles and self-propelled artillery, and 26 types of field artillery and rocket launchers.

## Casualties

Main article: [Vietnam War casualties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War_casualties)

See also: [Vietnam War body count controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War_body_count_controversy)

| Year | US[^cite_note-USarchives-395] | South Vietnam |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1956–1959 | 4 | n.a. |
| 1960 | 5 | 2,223 |
| 1961 | 16 | 4,004 |
| 1962 | 53 | 4,457 |
| 1963 | 122 | 5,665 |
| 1964 | 216 | 7,457 |
| 1965 | 1,928 | 11,242 |
| 1966 | 6,350 | 11,953 |
| 1967 | 11,363 | 12,716 |
| 1968 | 16,899 | 27,915 |
| 1969 | 11,780 | 21,833 |
| 1970 | 6,173 | 23,346 |
| 1971 | 2,414 | 22,738 |
| 1972 | 759 | 39,587 |
| 1973 | 68 | 27,901 |
| 1974 | 1 | 31,219 |
| 1975 | 62 | n.a. |
| After 1975 | 7 | n.a. |
| Total | 58,220 | >254,256[^cite_note-Clarke-396]<sup>: 275 </sup> |

Casualty estimates vary, with one source suggesting up to 3.8 million violent war deaths in Vietnam between 1955 and 2002.[^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399][^cite_note-Toledo_Blade_320,000_Chinese_troops-5] A demographic study calculated 791,000–1,141,000 war-related deaths for all Vietnam, military and civilians.[^cite_note-Hirschman-18] Between 195,000 and 430,000 South Vietnamese civilians died.[^cite_note-Lewy-19]<sup>: 450–453 </sup>[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThayer1985\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_May_2025\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (May_2025)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-28] Guenter Lewy estimated 65,000 North Vietnamese civilians died.[^cite_note-Lewy-19]<sup>: 450–453 </sup> Estimates of civilian deaths caused by American bombing of North Vietnam range from 30,000[^cite_note-Tucker-8]<sup>: 176, 617 </sup> to 182,000.[^cite_note-bfvietnam-20] A 1975 US Senate subcommittee estimated 1.4 million South Vietnamese civilians casualties, including 415,000 deaths.[^cite_note-Turse-357]<sup>: 12 </sup> The military of South Vietnam suffered an estimated 254,000 killed between 1960 and 1974, and additional deaths between 1954 and 1959, and in 1975.[^cite_note-Clarke-396]<sup>: 275 </sup> Other estimates point to higher figures of 313,000 casualties.[^cite_note-Obermeyer-43][^cite_note-Hirschman-18][^cite_note-Heuveline-44][^cite_note-Banister-45][^cite_note-Sliwinski-46]

The US Department of Defense figure for PAVN/VC killed from 1965 to 1974 was 950,765. Officials believed these need to be deflated by 30 percent. Lewy asserts that one-third of the reported "enemy" killed may have been civilians, concluding the figure was closer to 440,000.[^cite_note-Lewy-19]<sup>: 450–453 </sup>

According to Vietnamese government figures in 2013, there were 849,018 confirmed military deaths on the PAVN/VC side,[^cite_note-Chuyen-22] for the period of 1955–1975. An additional 232,000 personnel were estimated to be missing, and 463,000 were estimated to be wounded.[^cite_note-Moyar,_Mark-21] According to a 2015 official publication, communist forces had an estimated 1.1 million deaths and 300,000 personnel missing during the war.[^cite_note-Numbers-400]<sup>: 280 </sup>

US reports of "enemy KIA", referred to as body count, were subject to "falsification and glorification", and a true estimate of PAVN/VC combat deaths is difficult to assess, as US victories were assessed by having a "greater kill ratio".[^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402] It was difficult to distinguish between civilians and military personnel in the VC, as many were part-time guerrillas or impressed laborers who did not wear uniforms[^cite_note-403]<sup>: 32 </sup>[^cite_note-404] and civilians killed were sometimes written off as enemy killed, because high enemy casualties was directly tied to promotions and commendation.[^cite_note-Currey-269]<sup>: 649–650 </sup>[^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406]

Between 275,000[^cite_note-Banister-45]–310,000[^cite_note-Sliwinski-46] Cambodians died, including 50,000–150,000 combatants and civilians from US bombings.[^cite_note-407] 20,000–62,000 Laotians died,[^cite_note-Obermeyer-43] and 58,281 US military personnel were killed,[^cite_note-2new-31] of which 1,584 are still listed missing as of 2021.[^cite_note-408]

## Aftermath

### In Southeast Asia

#### In Vietnam

Further information: [Re-education camp (Vietnam)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Re-education_camp_\(Vietnam\)) and [Subsidy period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsidy_period)



B-52 wreckage in Huu Tiep Lake, Hanoi. Downed during [Operation Linebacker II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Linebacker_II), its remains have been turned into [a war monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B-52_Victory_Museum,_Hanoi).

In July 1976, North and South Vietnam were merged to form the Socialist Republic of Vietnam.[^cite_note-409] Despite speculation the victorious North Vietnamese would, in Nixon's words, "massacre the civilians there \[South Vietnam] by the millions", no mass executions happened.[^cite_note-Elliot2010-410]<sup>: 499, 512–513 </sup>[^cite_note-413]



Vietnamese refugees fleeing Vietnam, 1984

However, many South Vietnamese were sent to [re-education camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Re-education_camp_\(Vietnam\)) where they endured torture, starvation, and disease while forced to perform hard labor.[^cite_note-414][^cite_note-415] The number involved varied depending on different observers: "50,000 to 80,000" (*Le Monde*, 1978), "150,000 to 200,000" (*The Washington Post*, 1978), and "300,000" (Agence France Presse from Hanoi, 1978).[^cite_note-416] Such variations are because "Some estimates may include not only detainees but also people sent from the cities to the countryside." According to a native observer, 443,360 people had to register for a period in re-education camps in Saigon alone, and while some were released after a few days, others stayed for more than a decade.[^cite_note-417] Between 1975 and 1980, more than 1 million northerners migrated south, to regions formerly in the Republic of Vietnam, while, as part of the [New Economic Zones program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Economic_Zones_program), around 750,000 to over 1 million southerners were moved to mountainous forested areas.[^cite_note-Desbarats-418][^cite_note-Chapman-419] [Gabriel García Márquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Garc%C3%ADa_M%C3%A1rquez) described South Vietnam as a "False paradise" when he visited in 1980:

> The cost of this delirium was stupefying: 360,000 people mutilated, a million widows, 500,000 prostitutes, 500,000 drug addicts, a million tuberculous and more than a million soldiers of the old regime, impossible to rehabilitate into a new society. Ten percent of the population of Ho Chi Minh City was suffering from serious venereal diseases when the war ended, and there were 4 million illiterates throughout the South.[^cite_note-420]

The US used its [security council veto](1900272.md) to block Vietnam's UN recognition three times, an obstacle to it receiving aid.[^cite_note-421]

#### Cambodia and Laos

See also: [Mayaguez incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayaguez_incident)

By 1975, the North Vietnamese had lost influence over the Khmer Rouge.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings2018708-422] [Phnom Penh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phnom_Penh), Cambodia's capital, fell to the Khmer Rouge. Under [Pol Pot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pol_Pot), the Khmer Rouge would [kill between one million and three million Cambodians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_genocide) from a population of 8 million.[^cite_note-Heuveline-44][^cite_note-423][^cite_note-424][^cite_note-425]

The relationship between Vietnam and [Democratic Kampuchea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Kampuchea) deteriorated. In response to the Khmer Rouge taking [Phu Quoc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phu_Quoc) and [Tho Chu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%E1%BB%95_Ch%C3%A2u_Island), and the belief they were responsible for the disappearance of 500 Vietnamese natives on Tho Chu, Vietnam launched a counterattack to recover the islands.[^cite_note-426] After failed attempts to negotiate, Vietnam invaded Democratic Kampuchea in 1978 and ousted the Khmer Rouge, in the [Cambodian–Vietnamese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian%E2%80%93Vietnamese_War). In response, China invaded Vietnam in 1979. The two countries fought the [Sino-Vietnamese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Vietnamese_War). From 1978 to 1979, some 450,000 ethnic [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoa_people) left Vietnam by boat as refugees, or were deported.

The Pathet Lao overthrew the monarchy of Laos in 1975, establishing the [Lao People's Democratic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lao_People's_Democratic_Republic). The change in regime was "quite peaceful, a sort of Asiatic '[velvet revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Velvet_revolution)'"—although 30,000 former officials were sent to reeducation camps, often enduring harsh conditions.[^cite_note-Courtois-119]<sup>: 575–576 </sup>

#### Unexploded ordnance

See also: [Operation Rolling Thunder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Rolling_Thunder), [Operation Menu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Menu), and [Operation Freedom Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Freedom_Deal)

The US dropped over 7 million tons of bombs on Indochina during the war, more than triple the 2.1 million tons it dropped on Europe and Asia during World War II, and ten times the amount during the [Korean War](16772.md). 500 thousand tons were dropped on Cambodia, 1 million tons on North Vietnam, and 4 million tons on South Vietnam. On a per person basis, the 2 million tons dropped on Laos make it the most heavily bombed country in history, "nearly a ton for every person in Laos".[^cite_note-KiernanTaylor-206] Due to the particularly heavy impact of cluster bombs, Laos was a strong advocate of the [Convention on Cluster Munitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_Cluster_Munitions) to ban them, and host to its first meeting in 2010.[^cite_note-427]

Former US Air Force official Earl Tilford recounted "repeated bombing runs of a lake in central Cambodia. The B-52s literally dropped their payloads in the lake." The Air Force ran many missions like this to secure additional funding during budget negotiations, so the tonnage expended does not directly correlate with the resulting damage.[^cite_note-428]

[Unexploded ordnance](406804.md), mostly from US bombing, continues to kill, and has rendered much land hazardous and impossible to cultivate. Ordnance has killed 42,000 people since the war.[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430] In Laos, 80 million unexploded bombs remain. Unexploded ordnance has killed or injured over 20,000 Laotians and about 50 people are killed or maimed annually.[^cite_note-Wright-431][^cite_note-432] It is estimated the explosives will not be removed entirely for centuries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENguyen2012317-433]

#### Refugee crisis

Main articles: [Indochina refugee crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indochina_refugee_crisis), [Vietnamese boat people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_boat_people), and [Vietnamese diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_diaspora)

Over 3 million people left Vietnam, Laos, and Cambodia in the [Indochina refugee crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indochina_refugee_crisis) after 1975. Most Asian countries were unwilling to accept them, many fled by boat and were known as [boat people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boat_people).[^cite_note-434] Between 1975 and 1998, an estimated 1.2 million [refugees](45547.md) from Vietnam and other Southeast Asian countries resettled in the US, while Canada, Australia, and France resettled over 500,000, China accepted 250,000.[^cite_note-435] Laos experienced the largest flight proportionally, 300,000 out of a population of 3 million crossed the border into Thailand. Included among them were "about 90%" of Laos' "intellectuals, technicians, and officials".[^cite_note-Courtois-119]<sup>: 575 </sup> According to author Nghia M. Vo and the [United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees](31964.md) (UNHCR), between 200,000 and 250,000 boat people died at sea.[^cite_note-Vo167-436][^cite_note-Henningfeld-58]

### In the United States

Main article: [United States in the Vietnam War](8453309.md)



A young [Marine](17349325.md) private waits on the beach during the Marine landing, [Da Nang](425140.md), 3 August 1965

Failure of US goals is placed at different institutions and levels. Some have suggested it was due to failure of leadership.[^cite_note-437] Others point to military doctrine. Secretary of Defense Robert McNamara stated that "the achievement of a military victory by U.S. forces ... was indeed a dangerous illusion".[^cite_note-McNamara-102]<sup>: 368 </sup> The inability to bring Hanoi to the bargaining table by bombing illustrated another US miscalculation, and the limitations of military abilities in achieving political goals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnow199717-438] [Army Chief of Staff](485507.md) [Harold Keith Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Keith_Johnson) noted, "if anything came out of Vietnam, it was that air power couldn't do the job".[^cite_note-Buzzano-439] General William Westmoreland admitted bombing had been ineffective, saying he doubted "that the North Vietnamese would have relented".[^cite_note-Buzzano-439] Kissinger wrote to President Ford that "in terms of military tactics ... our armed forces are not suited to this kind of war. Even the Special Forces who had been designed for it could not prevail."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKissinger1975-440] Hanoi persistently sought unification under communist rule, and the effects of US bombing had negligible impact on their goals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENguyen20121–10-441] US bombing mobilized people throughout North Vietnam and internationally, due to a superpower attempting to bomb a society into submission.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENguyen201248–52-442]

Americans struggled to absorb the lessons of the military intervention. President [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) coined the term "[Vietnam syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_syndrome)" to describe the reluctance of the public and politicians to support military interventions abroad. US polling in 1978 revealed nearly 72% of Americans believed the war was "fundamentally wrong and immoral".[^cite_note-Hagopian-333]<sup>: 10 </sup> Six months after the beginning of Operation Rolling Thunder, [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup,_Inc.) found 60% did not believe sending troops was a mistake in September 1965, and only 24% believed it was. Polling did not find a plurality believing it was a mistake until October 1967, and did not find a majority believing it was until August 1968, during the third phase of the Tet Offensive. Thereafter, Gallup found majorities believing it was a mistake through the signing of the Peace Accords in 1973, when 60% believed it was a mistake, and polls between 1990 and 2000, found 70% of Americans believed it was a mistake.[^cite_note-443] The [Vietnam War POW/MIA issue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War_POW/MIA_issue), concerning the fate of US service personnel [missing in action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missing_in_action), persisted. The costs loom large in American consciousness; a 1990 poll showed the public incorrectly believed more Americans died in Vietnam than World War II.[^cite_note-444]

#### Financial cost

| Military costs | Military aid | Economic aid | Total | Total (2015 dollars) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| \$111 billion | \$16 billion | \$7 billion | \$135 billion | \$1 trillion |

Between 1953 and 1975, the US was estimated to have spent \$168 billion on the war (equivalent to \$1.7 trillion in 2025).[^cite_note-446] This resulted in a large [budget deficit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budget_deficit). Other figures point to \$139 billion from 1965 to 1974 (not inflation-adjusted), 10 times education spending, and 50 times more than housing and community development.[^cite_note-CQ-447] It was stated that war-spending could have paid every mortgage in the US, with money left over.[^cite_note-CQ-447] As of 2013, the US government pays Vietnam veterans and their families more than \$22 billion annually in war-related claims.[^cite_note-448][^cite_note-449]

#### Impact on the US military

See also: [Vietnam War resisters in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War_resisters_in_Canada) and [Vietnam War resisters in Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War_resisters_in_Sweden)



A marine gets his wounds treated during operations in Huế City, in 1968

More than 3 million Americans served, 1.5 million saw combat.[^cite_note-450] "At the height of American involvement in 1968, for example, 543,400 American military personnel were stationed in Vietnam, but only 80,000 were ... combat troops."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWestheider200778-451] Conscription existed since World War II, but ended in 1973.[^cite_note-bbmdst-452][^cite_note-mdebld-453]

A total of 58,220 American soldiers were killed,[^cite_note-USd\&w-33] 150,000 wounded, and at least 21,000 permanently disabled.[^cite_note-DigitalHistory-454] The average age of troops killed was 23.[^cite_note-455][^cite_note-Kueter-456] Approximately 830,000 veterans, 15%, suffered [posttraumatic stress disorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Posttraumatic_stress_disorder).[^cite_note-DigitalHistory-454][^cite_note-457] Drug use, racial tensions, and fragging—attempts to kill officers—created problems for the military and impacted its capability,[^cite_note-Lepre-458]<sup>: 44–47 </sup> as did the 125,000 Americans who left for Canada to avoid the draft,[^cite_note-459] and the approximately 50,000 servicemen who deserted.[^cite_note-460] In 1977, President [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) granted an unconditional pardon to Vietnam [draft evaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Draft_evasion_in_the_Vietnam_War) with [Proclamation 4483](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_4483).[^cite_note-461]

The war called into question army doctrine. Marine general [Victor H. Krulak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_H._Krulak) criticized Westmoreland's attrition strategy, calling it "wasteful of American lives ... with small likelihood of a successful outcome".[^cite_note-Buzzano-439] Doubts surfaced about the military's ability to train foreign forces. There were found to be considerable flaws and dishonesty by commanders, due to promotions being tied to the body count system touted by Westmoreland and McNamara.[^cite_note-Mohr-220] Secretary of Defense McNamara wrote to President Johnson: "The picture of the world's greatest superpower killing or seriously injuring 1,000 noncombatants a week, while trying to pound a tiny backward nation into submission on an issue whose merits are hotly disputed, is not a pretty one."[^cite_note-462]

### Effects of US chemical defoliation

Further information: [Environmental impact of the Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_impact_of_the_Vietnam_War)



US helicopter spraying chemical [defoliants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defoliant) in the [Mekong Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mekong_Delta), South Vietnam, 1969

|  | It has been suggested that portions of this article be [**split**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Splitting) out into another article titled *[Environmental impact of the Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_impact_of_the_Vietnam_War)*. ([**Discuss**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Vietnam_War)) *(July 2026)* |
| --- | --- |

Another controversial aspect of the US effort was use of chemical [defoliants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defoliant) between 1961 and 1971. 20 million gallons of toxic herbicides, like [Agent Orange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agent_Orange), were sprayed on 6 million acres of forests and crops.[^cite_note-Westing-1984-56][^cite_note-463] They were used to [defoliate](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/defoliate) parts of the countryside to prevent the VC from hiding weaponry and encampments, and deprive them of food. Defoliation was used to clear sensitive areas, including base perimeters and ambush sites along roads and canals.[^cite_note-Lewy-19]<sup>: 263 </sup> The chemicals used continue to change the landscape, cause diseases and birth defects, and poison the foodchain,[^cite_note-464][^cite_note-465][^cite_note-466] including suppressing the growth of some plants and crops and proceeding into the sediment, affecting fish and other species.[^cite_note-467] US records have listed figures including the destruction of 20% of the jungles of South Vietnam and 20-36% of the [mangrove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangrove) forests.[^cite_note-Fox-2003-468] The environmental destruction caused was described by Swedish Prime Minister [Olof Palme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olof_Palme), lawyers, and academics as an [ecocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecocide).[^cite_note-Zierler-2011-469][^cite_note-470][^cite_note-Falk-1973-471][^cite_note-472][^cite_note-Chiarini-2022-473][^cite_note-474]

The chemicals in Agent Orange[^cite_note-475] pose health hazards, such as immune system disorders, developmental abnormalities, and issues with the reproductive system.[^cite_note-476] Agent Orange and similar substances have caused many health issues for Vietnamese people and the US crews that handled them. Reports concluded that refugees exposed to sprays continued to experience pain in the eyes, skin and gastrointestinal upsets. In one study, 92% of participants suffered incessant fatigue; others reported [monstrous births](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monstrous_birth).[^cite_note-477] Analysis has found significant correlation between having a parent who was exposed to Agent Orange with the likelihood of possessing or acting as a carrier of birth defects.[^cite_note-478] The most common deformity appears to be [spina bifida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spina_bifida). There is substantial evidence defects carry on for three generations or more.[^cite_note-479] In 2012, the US and Vietnam cooperated in cleaning toxic chemicals on [Da Nang International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da_Nang_International_Airport), marking the first time Washington has been involved in cleaning up Agent Orange in Vietnam.[^cite_note-480] In 2018, Vietnam treated 150,000 cubic meters of contaminated soil.[^cite_note-481]



Handicapped children in Vietnam, most of them victims of [Agent Orange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agent_Orange), 2004

Vietnamese victims attempted a class action lawsuit against [Dow Chemical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dow_Chemical) and other US chemical manufacturers, but a [US District Court](882402.md) dismissed their case.[^cite_note-482][^cite_note-483] As of 2006, the Vietnamese government estimated there were over 4,000,000 victims of [dioxin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dioxins_and_dioxin-like_compounds) poisoning in Vietnam, while the [Vietnamese Red Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_Red_Cross) estimates up to one million people have health problems or disabilities as a result of Agent Orange.[^cite_note-484] The US has described these figures as unreliable,[^cite_note-Tucker-8] and denied conclusive scientific links between Agent Orange and Vietnamese victims of dioxin poisoning. In parts of southern Vietnam, dioxin levels remain at over 100 times the accepted international standard.[^cite_note-485]

The US Veterans Administration has listed [prostate cancer](88078.md), [respiratory cancers](18450.md), [multiple myeloma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiple_myeloma), [type 2 diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_2_diabetes), [B-cell lymphomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B-cell_lymphomas), [soft-tissue sarcoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft-tissue_sarcoma), [chloracne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chloracne), [porphyria cutanea tarda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porphyria_cutanea_tarda), [peripheral neuropathy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peripheral_neuropathy) as "presumptive diseases associated with exposure to Agent Orange or other herbicides during military service".[^cite_note-486] Spina bifida is the sole birth defect in children of veterans recognized as caused by exposure to Agent Orange.[^cite_note-487]

### In popular culture

Main article: [List of Vietnam War films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Vietnam_War_films)



Stone plaque with photo of the *Thương tiếc* ("Mourning Soldier") statue, originally, installed at the [Republic of Vietnam National Military Cemetery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%ACnh_An_Cemetery). The original statue was demolished after April 1975.

The war has featured extensively in television, film, video games, music and literature. In Vietnam, the *[Girl from Hanoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Girl_from_Hanoi)* (1974) was a film set during Operation Linebacker II, depicting wartime life. Another notable work was the diary of [Đặng Thùy Trâm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%90%E1%BA%B7ng_Th%C3%B9y_Tr%C3%A2m), a doctor killed by US forces at age 27. It was published in English as *Last Night I Dreamed of Peace* and adapted into the film *[Don't Burn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_Burn)*.[^cite_note-488] The novel *[The Sorrow of War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sorrow_of_War)* by North Vietnamese veteran [Bảo Ninh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%E1%BA%A3o_Ninh) was controversial in Vietnam but became an international bestseller.[^cite_note-489][^cite_note-490]

The first major film on the war was [John Wayne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Wayne)'s pro-war *[The Green Berets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Green_Berets_\(film\))* (1968). Further films were released, the most noteworthy examples being [Michael Cimino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Cimino)'s *[The Deer Hunter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Deer_Hunter)* (1978), [Francis Ford Coppola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Ford_Coppola)'s *[Apocalypse Now](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apocalypse_Now)* (1979), [Oliver Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Stone)'s *[Platoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platoon_\(film\))* (1986), and [Stanley Kubrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Kubrick)'s *[Full Metal Jacket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_Metal_Jacket)* (1987). Other films include *[Good Morning, Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Morning,_Vietnam)* (1987), *[Casualties of War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casualties_of_War)* (1989), and *[Born on the Fourth of July](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Born_on_the_Fourth_of_July_\(film\))* (1989).

The war influenced a generation of musicians and songwriters, pro/anti-war and pro/anti-communist. The [Vietnam War Song Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War_Song_Project) has identified 5,000+ songs referencing the conflict.[^cite_note-491] [Country Joe and the Fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_Joe_and_the_Fish) recorded "[I-Feel-Like-I'm-Fixin'-to-Die Rag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_%22Fish%22_Cheer/I-Feel-Like-I'm-Fixin'-to-Die_Rag)" in 1965, and it became one of the most influential protest anthems.[^cite_note-Tucker-8]

#### Myths

See also: [Myth of the spat-on Vietnam veteran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myth_of_the_spat-on_Vietnam_veteran) and [Vietnam stab-in-the-back myth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_stab-in-the-back_myth)

Myths play a role in the [historiography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography) of the war. Discussion of myth has focused on US experiences, but changing myths have played a role in Vietnamese and Australian historiography. Scholarship has focused on "myth-busting",[^cite_note-Milam-492]<sup>: 373 </sup> attacking orthodox and revisionist schools of historiography, and challenging myths about American society and soldiery in the war.[^cite_note-Milam-492]<sup>: 373 </sup>

Kuzmarov in *The Myth of the Addicted Army: Vietnam and the Modern War on Drugs* challenges the popular and Hollywood narrative that US soldiers were heavy drug users,[^cite_note-493] in particular the notion that the Mỹ Lai massacre was caused by drug use.[^cite_note-Milam-492]<sup>: 373 </sup> According to Kuzmarov, Nixon is primarily responsible for creating the drug myth.[^cite_note-Milam-492]<sup>: 374 </sup> Michael Allen accuses Nixon of mythmaking, by exploiting the plight of the [National League of POW/MIA Families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_League_of_POW/MIA_Families) to allow the government to appear caring, as the war was increasingly considered lost.[^cite_note-Milam-492]<sup>: 376 </sup> Allen's analysis ties the position of potential missing Americans, or prisoners into post-war politics and presidential elections, including the [Swift boat controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swift_Vets_and_POWs_for_Truth).[^cite_note-Milam-492]<sup>: 376–377 </sup>
